# 4:43: 45 questions

## 4:43/q01 Who is addressed as believers?  [يَٰٓأَيُّهَا ٱلَّذِينَ ءَامَنُوا۟ · referent]
turns on: The cited interpretations, reports, language arguments, and legal evidence differ.
- 4:43/q01/p1 the poem attributed to ibn rawāḥa praises the prophet as a messenger reciting scripture and bringing guidance in which believers trust — QURTUBI-FULL, d. 671 (عبد الله بن رواحة): The poem attributed to Ibn Rawāḥa praises the Prophet as a messenger reciting scripture and bringing guidance in which believers trust. JISHUMI (source note JISHUMI:v2p1567/r1) supports this position. [2 notes: QURTUBI-FULL(عبد الله بن رواحة); JISHUMI]
- 4:43/q01/p2 believers are addressed because they performed prayer despite having drunk and impaired their minds; disbelievers did not perform it, sober or intoxicated — QURTUBI-FULL, d. 671 (author): Believers are addressed because they performed prayer despite having drunk and impaired their minds; disbelievers did not perform it, sober or intoxicated. [1 notes: QURTUBI-FULL]
- 4:43/q01/p3 the concession is explained by god’s stated wish not to impose hardship on believers — BIQAI-FULL, d. 885 (author): The concession is explained by God’s stated wish not to impose hardship on believers. | mentions 5:6 [1 notes: BIQAI-FULL]
- 4:43/q01/p4 the imam infers the hypocrisy warning from the address to believers: one who defies that address is not truly a believer — TABATABAI, d. 1402 (author): The Imam infers the hypocrisy warning from the address to believers: one who defies that address is not truly a believer. [1 notes: TABATABAI]

## 4:43/q02 Does prayer here mean the ritual act, the mosque, or both?  [ٱلصَّلَوٰةَ · referent]
turns on: The cited interpretations, reports, language arguments, and legal evidence differ.
- 4:43/q02/p1 Prayer and mosque — RAZI-FULL, d. 606 (author): The mosque reading answers that people ordinarily go to the mosque for prayer, so whatever prevents prayer also counts as a reason not to go to the mosque. RAZI-FULL, d. 606 (ابن عباس وابن مسعود والحسن والشافعي): Ibn ʿAbbās, Ibn Masʿūd, and al-Ḥasan take ‘prayer’ to mean the mosque, and al-Shāfiʿī adopts this view; it can mean ‘place of prayer’ by common ellipsis, as ṣalawāt means prayer sites at 22:40. | mentions 22:40 QURTUBI-FULL, d. 671 (طائفة): Another group takes the wording to include both prayer and its place, since people then went to the mosque only for prayer and prayed only in congregation. BAYDAWI-FULL, d. 685 (الشافعي): If prayer means mosques, those passing through are allowed to cross while junub; al-Shāfiʿī holds this view. NASAFI-FULL, d. 710 (الشافعى): Al-Shafiʿi takes prayer to mean mosques: a junub person may not enter one except to pass through when needed. BIQAI-FULL, d. 885 (الشافعي): Al-Shāfiʿī takes prayer to mean both the prayer itself and its place, the mosque: the verse bars an intoxicated person from praying and a sexually impure person from being in the mosque. ALUSI-FULL, d. 1270 (الضحاك وعكرمة والحسن): Al-Dahhak, Ikrima, and al-Hasan say prayer means its locations, by a state-for-place metaphor; the verse bars an intoxicated person from approaching a mosque out of reverence. ALUSI-FULL, d. 1270 (الشافعي): Al-Shafi'i applies prayer to both the ritual act and its locations to accommodate both interpretations; al-Alusi rejects this because it combines literal and metaphorical senses. ELMALILI, d. 1361 (بعض المفسرين): Some commentators take salat here to mean the mosque or prayer-place, so the prohibition bars drunk people from entering mosques. KURANYOLU-TEFSIR (author): On the mosque-passage interpretation, prayer means the act of worship for the intoxicated, but the mosque as the place of prayer for the junub. FARRA (source note FARRA:v1p268/r10) supports this position. MAJAZ (source note MAJAZ:v1p126/r8) supports this position. TAB-FULL (source note TAB-FULL:v7p53/r7) supports this position. IBNABIHATIM (source note IBNABIHATIM:v3p959/r1) supports this position. WAHIDI-WAJIZ (source note WAHIDI-WAJIZ:4:43-44/r1) supports this position. JISHUMI (source note JISHUMI:v2p1567/r2) supports this position. JISHUMI (source note JISHUMI:v2p1567/r3) supports this position. KASHSHAF-FULL (source note KASHSHAF-FULL:v1p545#2/r3) supports this position. IBNATIYYA-FULL (source note IBNATIYYA-FULL:v2p56#2/r8) supports this position. IBNATIYYA-FULL (source note IBNATIYYA-FULL:v2p56#2/r9) supports this position. [20 notes: RAZI-FULL(ابن عباس وابن مسعود والحسن والشافعي); QURTUBI-FULL(طائفة); BAYDAWI-FULL(الشافعي); NASAFI-FULL(الشافعى); BIQAI-FULL(الشافعي); ALUSI-FULL(الشافعي,الضحاك وعكرمة والحسن); ELMALILI(بعض المفسرين); KURANYOLU-TEFSIR; FARRA; MAJAZ; TAB-FULL(آخرون); IBNABIHATIM(ابن عباس); WAHIDI-WAJIZ; JISHUMI(ابن عباس وسعيد بن جبير ومجاهد وابن زيد وأبو مسلم,عبد الله وسعيد بن المسيب والضحاك وعكرمة وعطاء والنخعي والحسن); KASHSHAF-FULL(قيل); IBNATIYYA-FULL(طائفة)]
- 4:43/q02/p2 Ritual prayer — IBNJAWZI-ZAD, d. 597 (علي، مجاهد، الحكم، قتادة، ابن زيد، مقاتل، الفراء، الزجاج): One interpretation permits a traveler without water to perform tayammum and pray while junub; on this view, passing by means being a traveler and approaching prayer means performing it. RAZI-FULL, d. 606 (author): If ṣalāt means the prayer, the verse bars praying drunk or junub except for a traveling wayfarer, allowing a junub traveler unable to find water to pray by tayammum. RAZI-FULL, d. 606 (الأكثرون): The majority take ṣalāt here to mean the prayer itself: do not perform it while intoxicated. QURTUBI-FULL, d. 671 (أبو حنيفة): Abu Hanifa takes 'prayer' to mean the ritual act itself, with 'until you know what you say' supporting that reading. SAMIN-DURR, d. 756 (غير مسمى): Alternatively, there is no ellipsis: the prohibition concerns approaching prayer itself in this state. BURSEVI, d. 1127 (التيسير): Taysir says the prayer itself is not forbidden, since it is worship; rather, one must not become intoxicated so as to be unable to pray properly. ALUSI-FULL, d. 1270 (author): Most interpreters understand prayer as the specific ritual act; approaching it means rising to perform it and entering into it, with the wording intensifying the prohibition. [7 notes: IBNJAWZI-ZAD(علي، مجاهد، الحكم، قتادة، ابن زيد، مقاتل، الفراء، الزجاج); RAZI-FULL(الأكثرون); QURTUBI-FULL(أبو حنيفة); SAMIN-DURR(غير مسمى); BURSEVI(التيسير); ALUSI-FULL]
- 4:43/q02/p3 Mosque or prayer place — RAZI-FULL, d. 606 (author): If ṣalāt means the mosque, the verse bars entering it drunk or while junub except to pass through, allowing a junub person to cross the mosque, as al-Shāfiʿī holds. RAZI-FULL, d. 606 (أصحاب الشافعي): Al-Shāfiʿī’s adherents prefer the mosque reading because ‘approach’ naturally applies to a place, whereas it does not literally apply to the prayer itself. RAZI-FULL, d. 606 (أصحاب الشافعي): They argue the exception works under the mosque reading; under the prayer reading it fails because a sick nontraveler unable to use water may still pray by tayammum. BURHAN, d. 794 (author): In the prohibition on approaching prayer while intoxicated or impure, ṣalāt means the prayer itself in “until you know what you say,” but its place in “except as passersby.” BIQAI-FULL, d. 885 (author): “Do not approach prayer” means do not enter the place where it is performed, much less perform the prayer itself. ALUSI-FULL, d. 1270 (author): The mosque reading is supported by a report that mosques should be kept away from children and madmen, but the phrase about knowing what one says weighs against it. STUDYQURAN (author): Ṣalāh or ṣalawāt can mean the place of prayer as well as prayer itself. | mentions 22:40 STUDYQURAN (Q): Clay can serve as a place of prayer, and its elements, water and earth, purify ritually; fire instead is a means of punishment in the Hereafter. | mentions 4:43, 5:6 [8 notes: RAZI-FULL; BURHAN; BIQAI-FULL; ALUSI-FULL; STUDYQURAN(Q); RAZI-FULL(أصحاب الشافعي)+]

- 4:43/q03/p1 in the account related through isra'il from abu ishaq and amr ibn shurahbil, umar says the verse was revealed and the prophet's caller announced that no drunk person should approach prayer; abu dawud transmits this — IBNKATHIR-FULL, d. 774 (عمر بن الخطاب): In the account related through Isra'il from Abu Ishaq and Amr ibn Shurahbil, Umar says the verse was revealed and the Prophet's caller announced that no drunk person should approach prayer; Abu Dawud transmits this wording. MAWARDI-FULL (source note MAWARDI-FULL:v1p489/r5) supports this position. MAWARDI-FULL (source note MAWARDI-FULL:v1p489/r6) supports this position. DAMGHANI (source note DAMGHANI:v1p374/r13) supports this position. KASHSHAF-FULL (source note KASHSHAF-FULL:v1p545#2/r2) supports this position. [5 notes: IBNKATHIR-FULL(عمر بن الخطاب); MAWARDI-FULL; DAMGHANI; KASHSHAF-FULL]

## 4:43/q05 What does intoxicated mean here?  [سُكَٰرَىٰ · meaning]
turns on: The cited interpretations, reports, language arguments, and legal evidence differ.
- 4:43/q05/p1 Wine intoxication — IBNJAWZI-ZAD, d. 597 (الجمهور): Most exegetes say the intoxication is from wine. RAZI-FULL, d. 606 (جمهور الصحابة والتابعين): The majority of Companions and Successors understand the word as intoxication from wine, the opposite of sobriety. QURTUBI-FULL, d. 671 (الجمهور): The majority of scholars and jurists understand the intoxication here as intoxication from wine. ABUHAYYAN-FULL, d. 745 (author): A drinking poem plays on السكر as a stoppage or drunkenness in the phrase نداوي السكر بالسكر. ABUHAYYAN-FULL, d. 745 (author): The command follows worship, filial kindness, good conduct, condemnation of miserliness, and the afterlife digression: prayer needed protection from wine’s disruption. | mentions 4:36, 4:37, 4:42 ABUHAYYAN-FULL, d. 745 (الجمهور): The majority understand سكارى as intoxicated from wine. SAMIN-DURR, d. 756 (author): The root sense of السكر is closure: intoxication blocks the link between a person and reason; the term usually means loss of reason through drink, but can also describe anger, love, and similar causes. SAMIN-DURR, d. 756 (author): The cited line’s repeated سكر witnesses metaphorical intoxication by love alongside literal wine intoxication. BIQAI-FULL, d. 885 (author): Intoxication means loss of reason through wine or another cause; weakened reason lets Satan prompt the tongue toward verbal shirk even while the heart remains faithful, a statement that will cause anguish at judgment. STUDYQURAN (author): The four passages progress from wine as provision (16:67), to mixed benefit and sin (2:219), avoiding prayer while drunk here, and finally complete prohibition (5:90). | mentions 16:67, 2:219, 5:90 TAB-FULL (source note TAB-FULL:v7p45#2/r2) supports this position. TAB-FULL (source note TAB-FULL:v7p48/r5) supports this position. TAB-FULL (source note TAB-FULL:v7p48/r6) supports this position. TAB-FULL (source note TAB-FULL:v7p48/r7) supports this position. - 4:43/q05/p2 Sleep or drowsiness — TABRISI, d. 548 (الضحاك وأبو جعفر): Another view takes intoxication to mean sleepiness; Aisha reports the Prophet told a drowsy worshipper to leave in case he unknowingly prayed against himself. RAZI-FULL, d. 606 (النبي صلى الله عليه وسلم): The Prophet said that when someone grows drowsy during prayer, they should sleep until the drowsiness passes, lest they seek forgiveness while actually cursing themselves; al-Ḍaḥḥāk uses this to support the sleep interpretation. RAZI-FULL, d. 606 (author): Al-Rāzī prefers wine intoxication: sakr literally means drunkenness from wine, while intoxication from passion, anger, fear, or sleep is figurative and normally qualified; he cites 50:19 and 22:2. | mentions 50:19, 22:2 QURTUBI-FULL, d. 671 (الضحاك): Al-Dahhak understands the state as sleepiness, citing the Prophetic direction that a sleepy worshipper should rest until alert because he may not know what he says. QURTUBI-FULL, d. 671 (author): Al-Qurtubi says the sleep and urinary-need interpretations express a sound principle: prayer requires wholehearted attention, free of distractions such as sleep, urgency, hunger, or anything that occupies and unsettles the mind. ABUHAYYAN-FULL, d. 745 (author): سكر is blocked discernment from intoxicating drink, likened to an eye covered by sleep or a river whose channels are blocked. ABUHAYYAN-FULL, d. 745 (القرطبي): Al-Qurṭubī says prayer requires heart and body engagement, so sleep, urinary pressure, hunger, and other distractions should be removed. ABUHAYYAN-FULL, d. 745 (الضحاك): Al-Ḍaḥḥāk interprets the phrase as drowsy from sleep, citing the Prophet’s instruction to sleep if drowsiness impairs prayer. IBNKATHIR-FULL, d. 774 (الضحاك): Al-Dahhak interprets the intoxication here as sleepiness, not wine intoxication; Ibn Jarir and Ibn Abi Hatim transmit this view. ALUSI-FULL, d. 1270 (جعفر والضحاك وابن عباس): Ja'far, al-Dahhak, and one report from Ibn Abbas take intoxication here to mean overpowering sleep or drowsiness. ASAD-NOTES, d. 1412 (author): Sukr covers any mental disequilibrium, including drugs, giddiness, passion, or metaphorical drunkenness from sleep, not only alcohol. [11 notes: TABRISI(الضحاك وأبو جعفر); RAZI-FULL(النبي صلى الله عليه وسلم); QURTUBI-FULL(الضحاك); ABUHAYYAN-FULL(الضحاك,القرطبي); IBNKATHIR-FULL(الضحاك); ALUSI-FULL(جعفر والضحاك وابن عباس); ASAD-NOTES; RAZI-FULL+]
- 4:43/q05/p3 Mental impairment more broadly — TABRISI, d. 548 (author): Sukr derives from blocking a water channel; intoxication blocks the path of understanding, and sakrat al-mawt means death’s swoon. TABRISI, d. 548 (author): The forms for a man are sakran and plural sukara; a woman is also described as sukra. QURTUBI-FULL, d. 671 (author): 'While you are intoxicated' is a subject-predicate clause functioning circumstantially with 'do not approach'; سكارى is a plural of سكران, like كسالى from كسلان. QURTUBI-FULL, d. 671 (النخعي): Al-Nakha'i reads سكرى with an open sin on the pattern فعلى, a broken plural of سكران, by analogy with صرعى because intoxication is an affliction of the mind. QURTUBI-FULL, d. 671 (الأعمش): Al-A'mash reads سكرى like حبلى as a singular adjective, which may describe a plural group. ABUHAYYAN-FULL, d. 745 (النخعي): Al-Nakhaʿī reads سكرى; it may be a feminine singular adjective applied to a group, or a plural of سكران on فعلى. SAMIN-DURR, d. 756 (author): Another usage takes السكر with damma and still kāf as a barrier; the cited poem uses it for being on and treating blockage with blockage. SAMIN-DURR, d. 756 (الأعمش): Al-A'mash reads سكرى with damma on sīn and a still kāf, construing it as a feminine singular adjective on فعلى applied to a group. BURSEVI, d. 1127 (author): Sukara is the plural of sakran, on the pattern of كسالى from كسلان. ALUSI-FULL, d. 1270 (الأعمش): Al-A'mash reads سكرى with a damma on the first letter, treating it as a feminine adjective describing a group. ALUSI-FULL, d. 1270 (النخعي): Al-Nakha'i reads سكرى with fatHa; it can be a singular adjective for a group or, according to Sibawayh, a broken plural, while others call it a collective noun; al-Alusi prefers Sibawayh's analysis. TABATABAI, d. 1402 (author): He favors the first chronology: the explicit prohibition in al-Baqara makes it unlikely that this later prayer-only restriction came afterward, unless intoxication means sluggishness in prayer as some reports say. | mentions 2:219 [12 notes: TABRISI; QURTUBI-FULL(الأعمش,النخعي); ABUHAYYAN-FULL(النخعي); SAMIN-DURR(الأعمش); BURSEVI; ALUSI-FULL(الأعمش,النخعي); TABATABAI+]

## 4:43/q06 What must an impaired worshipper know before praying?  [حَتَّىٰ تَعْلَمُوا۟ مَا تَقُولُونَ · interpretation]
turns on: The cited interpretations, reports, language arguments, and legal evidence differ.
- 4:43/q06/p1 the phrase is interpreted either as not exposing oneself to intoxication at prayer times or as not entering prayer while intoxicated; the author prefers the first because an intoxicated person cannot understand what is addressed — IBNJAWZI-ZAD, d. 597 (author): The phrase is interpreted either as not exposing oneself to intoxication at prayer times or as not entering prayer while intoxicated; the author prefers the first because an intoxicated person cannot understand what is addressed to him. IBNABIHATIM (source note IBNABIHATIM:v3p959/r4) supports this position. IBNATIYYA-FULL (source note IBNATIYYA-FULL:v2p56#2/r4) supports this position. [3 notes: IBNJAWZI-ZAD; IBNABIHATIM(سعيد بن جبير); IBNATIYYA-FULL]
- 4:43/q06/p2 another explanation is that a person in this state must avoid prayer, qurʾān recitation, touching the muṣḥaf, and entering a mosque — IBNJAWZI-ZAD, d. 597 (author): Another explanation is that a person in this state must avoid prayer, Qurʾān recitation, touching the muṣḥaf, and entering a mosque. [1 notes: IBNJAWZI-ZAD]
- 4:43/q06/p3 proponents of the prayer reading argue that ‘until you know what you say’ concerns prayer, which has prescribed speech, while no prescribed speech in the mosque is affected by drunkenness — RAZI-FULL, d. 606 (من نصر القول الثاني): Proponents of the prayer reading argue that ‘until you know what you say’ concerns prayer, which has prescribed speech, while no prescribed speech in the mosque is affected by drunkenness. [1 notes: RAZI-FULL(من نصر القول الثاني)]
- 4:43/q06/p4 qurtubi continues the tirmidhi report from ali: after abd al-rahman ibn awf served wine at a feast, intoxicated companions put ali forward for prayer, and he recited al-kafirun as 'we worship what you worship'; the — QURTUBI-FULL, d. 671 (author): Qurtubi continues the Tirmidhi report from Ali: after Abd al-Rahman ibn Awf served wine at a feast, intoxicated companions put Ali forward for prayer, and he recited al-Kafirun as 'we worship what you worship'; the verse followed. | mentions 109:2 [1 notes: QURTUBI-FULL]
- 4:43/q06/p5 abu isa al-tirmidhi grades ali's report about the drunken prayer and mistaken recitation حسن صحيح — QURTUBI-FULL, d. 671 (أبو عيسى): Abu Isa al-Tirmidhi grades Ali's report about the drunken prayer and mistaken recitation حسن صحيح. [1 notes: QURTUBI-FULL(أبو عيسى)]
- 4:43/q06/p6 the phrase means that the worshipper must know what he says with certainty and without error — QURTUBI-FULL, d. 671 (author): The phrase means that the worshipper must know what he says with certainty and without error. [1 notes: QURTUBI-FULL]
- 4:43/q06/p7 ibn al-mundhir takes confused recitation as evidence of intoxication; one who does not know what he says must avoid the mosque, has no valid prayer, and must make it up, while one who knows what — QURTUBI-FULL, d. 671 (ابن المنذر): Ibn al-Mundhir takes confused recitation as evidence of intoxication; one who does not know what he says must avoid the mosque, has no valid prayer, and must make it up, while one who knows what he says is treated as sober if he prays. [1 notes: QURTUBI-FULL(ابن المنذر)]
- 4:43/q06/p8 when wine was permitted, abd al-rahman ibn awf hosted companions who drank until drunk; at maghrib, one led prayer and recited a mistaken line, prompting this verse — BAYDAWI-FULL, d. 685 (عبد الرحمن بن عوف): When wine was permitted, Abd al-Rahman ibn Awf hosted Companions who drank until drunk; at Maghrib, one led prayer and recited a mistaken line, prompting this verse. [1 notes: BAYDAWI-FULL(عبد الرحمن بن عوف)]
- 4:43/q06/p9 do not approach prayer in a state of intoxication until you understand what you are reciting — NASAFI-FULL, d. 710 (author): Do not approach prayer in a state of intoxication until you understand what you are reciting. [1 notes: NASAFI-FULL]
- 4:43/q06/p10 abū ḥayyān prefers taking الصلاة literally as prayer; taking it as prayer places is figurative and should be used only when the literal sense cannot work. required recitation obstructs prayer while drunk, unlike mosque entry — ABUHAYYAN-FULL, d. 745 (author): Abū Ḥayyān prefers taking الصلاة literally as prayer; taking it as prayer places is figurative and should be used only when the literal sense cannot work. Required recitation obstructs prayer while drunk, unlike mosque entry. [1 notes: ABUHAYYAN-FULL+]
- 4:43/q06/p11 anas reports the prophet's instruction that a person who becomes drowsy in prayer should stop and sleep until able to know what he is saying; bukhari and al-nasa'i transmit it, and another wording warns he — IBNKATHIR-FULL, d. 774 (أنس): Anas reports the Prophet's instruction that a person who becomes drowsy in prayer should stop and sleep until able to know what he is saying; Bukhari and al-Nasa'i transmit it, and another wording warns he might curse himself while seeking forgiveness. [1 notes: IBNKATHIR-FULL(أنس)]
- 4:43/q06/p12 other transmitters clarify the mistaken recitation as “i worship what you worship”; al-tirmidhī’s version says, “and we worship what you worship.” — BIQAI-FULL, d. 885 (author): Other transmitters clarify the mistaken recitation as “I worship what you worship”; al-Tirmidhī’s version says, “and we worship what you worship.” | mentions 109:1 [1 notes: BIQAI-FULL]
- 4:43/q06/p13 the prohibition lasts until intoxication passes and people understand their words, preventing them from altering what they say in prayer — BIQAI-FULL, d. 885 (author): The prohibition lasts until intoxication passes and people understand their words, preventing them from altering what they say in prayer. [1 notes: BIQAI-FULL]
- 4:43/q06/p14 ali reports that after abd al-rahman ibn awf served wine at a meal, intoxicated ali led prayer and misrecited al-kafirun; verse 4:43 then barred prayer while drunk. al-tirmidhi graded the report hasan and al-hakim authenticated — DURR-FULL, d. 911 (علي بن أبي طالب): Ali reports that after Abd al-Rahman ibn Awf served wine at a meal, intoxicated Ali led prayer and misrecited al-Kafirun; verse 4:43 then barred prayer while drunk. Al-Tirmidhi graded the report hasan and al-Hakim authenticated it. | mentions 109:2 [1 notes: DURR-FULL(علي بن أبي طالب)]
- 4:43/q06/p15 ʿalī reports that ʿabd al-raḥmān b. ʿawf served wine at a meal; when intoxicated, ʿalī led maghrib and confused the recitation, prompting the prohibition on approaching prayer drunk — SUYUTI-LUBAB, d. 911 (علي): ʿAlī reports that ʿAbd al-Raḥmān b. ʿAwf served wine at a meal; when intoxicated, ʿAlī led Maghrib and confused the recitation, prompting the prohibition on approaching prayer drunk. | mentions 109:1-6 [1 notes: SUYUTI-LUBAB(علي)]
- 4:43/q06/p16 abu mansur explains that wording such as no prayer for a runaway slave or disobedient wife condemns the disobedience, not the prayer; intoxication likewise does not cancel the obligation, so pray only when aware of — BURSEVI, d. 1127 (أبو منصور): Abu Mansur explains that wording such as no prayer for a runaway slave or disobedient wife condemns the disobedience, not the prayer; intoxication likewise does not cancel the obligation, so pray only when aware of what will be recited. [1 notes: BURSEVI(أبو منصور)]
- 4:43/q06/p17 junub applies alike to masculine and feminine, singular and plural, because it functions like a verbal noun; janabah means distance, since it keeps one from recitation and prayer — BURSEVI, d. 1127 (author): Junub applies alike to masculine and feminine, singular and plural, because it functions like a verbal noun; janabah means distance, since it keeps one from recitation and prayer. [1 notes: BURSEVI]
- 4:43/q06/p18 do not pray while intoxicated until, before beginning, you know what you will say, which implies knowing what you will recite in prayer — ALUSI-FULL, d. 1270 (author): Do not pray while intoxicated until, before beginning, you know what you will say, which implies knowing what you will recite in prayer. [1 notes: ALUSI-FULL+]
- 4:43/q06/p19 ibn jubayr, as reported by ibn abi hatim, takes the phrase to mean being tipsy from drink until one knows what one recites in prayer — ALUSI-FULL, d. 1270 (ابن جبير): Ibn Jubayr, as reported by Ibn Abi Hatim, takes the phrase to mean being tipsy from drink until one knows what one recites in prayer. [1 notes: ALUSI-FULL(ابن جبير)]
- 4:43/q06/p20 “until you know what you say” gives the prohibition’s limit and hints at its rationale; impaired speech signals the earlier disruption of prayer’s actions as reason fails — IBNASHUR-FULL, d. 1393 (author): “Until you know what you say” gives the prohibition’s limit and hints at its rationale; impaired speech signals the earlier disruption of prayer’s actions as reason fails. [1 notes: IBNASHUR-FULL]
- 4:43/q06/p21 until you know what you say gives the reason for avoiding drink that leaves one intoxicated at prayer time; it is not an endpoint after which drinking to intoxication is allowed — TABATABAI, d. 1402 (author): Until you know what you say gives the reason for avoiding drink that leaves one intoxicated at prayer time; it is not an endpoint after which drinking to intoxication is allowed. [1 notes: TABATABAI]
- 4:43/q06/p22 an intoxicated person must wait until knowing what they say, and a person in major ritual impurity must wait until performing ghusl before prayer — KURANYOLU-TEFSIR (author): An intoxicated person must wait until knowing what they say, and a person in major ritual impurity must wait until performing ghusl before prayer. [1 notes: KURANYOLU-TEFSIR]
- 4:43/q06/p23 believers must not approach prayer, its mosque, or prepare for it while drunk; they may do so once sober and able to understand their recitation — STUDYQURAN (author): Believers must not approach prayer, its mosque, or prepare for it while drunk; they may do so once sober and able to understand their recitation. | mentions 22:40 [1 notes: STUDYQURAN]

## 4:43/q07 Was the restriction on prayer while drunk abrogated?  [وَأَنتُمْ سُكَٰرَىٰ · sciences]
turns on: The cited interpretations, reports, language arguments, and legal evidence differ.
- 4:43/q07/p1 Its earlier legal allowance was ended by the wine ban — TABRISI, d. 548 (ابن عباس ومجاهد وقتادة): The intoxication means drunkenness from wine, later abrogated by wine’s prohibition; the account is also related from Musa ibn Jaʿfar. RAZI-FULL, d. 606 (author): Some call the verse abrogated by al-Māʾida; al-Rāzī says the abrogated implication is that prayer becomes permitted once a drinker can understand, which the later total ban on wine removes. | mentions 5:90 QURTUBI-FULL, d. 671 (مجاهد وعكرمة وقتادة): Mujahid, Ikrima, and Qatada say the verse was abrogated by the final prohibition of wine; al-Qurtubi calls this the correct view because of Ali's report. | mentions 5:91 QURTUBI-FULL, d. 671 (عمر بن الخطاب): Abu Dawud's report from Umar presents alcohol prohibition in stages: after 2:219 and this restriction on prayer while drunk, the prohibition in 5:91 was revealed and Umar said they had stopped. | mentions 2:219, 5:91 ABUHAYYAN-FULL, d. 745 (ابن عباس): Ibn ʿAbbās considers the verse abrogated by the prohibition in Sūrat al-Māʾida. | mentions 5:90 ABUHAYYAN-FULL, d. 745 (ابن عباس): Ibn ʿAbbās argues that the address implies drinking was allowed, since it forbade only approaching prayer while drunk; the wine ban superseded that implication. | mentions 5:90 IBNKATHIR-FULL, d. 774 (author): Ibn Kathir places the restriction in the staged prohibition of wine: after 2:219, people avoided drinking at prayer times; after 4:43 came the final ban in 5:90–91, to which Umar replied that they had stopped. | mentions 2:219, 5:90, 5:91 ITQAN, d. 911 (ابن عمر): Al-Tayalisi transmits Ibn Umar's account of alcohol's three-stage prohibition: first 2:219 left people free to benefit, then 4:43 prohibited drinking near prayer, and finally 5:90 prompted the Prophet to declare it forbidden. | mentions 4:43, 5:90 ASAD-NOTES, d. 1412 (some of the commentators): Some commentators regard the ban on praying drunk as an initial stage of total intoxicant prohibition, later abrogated by 5:90. | mentions 5:90 STUDYQURAN (author): Jurisprudence treats 5:90 as the authoritative final ruling, based on Prophetic sayings and practice, abrogating the other passages’ legal implications. | mentions 16:67, 2:219, 5:90 STUDYQURAN (minority tradition): A minority tradition includes excessive sleepiness in drunkenness, since it impairs prayer; on this reading the verse was not wholly abrogated by the wine ban. | mentions 5:90 ABDURRAZZAQ (source note ABDURRAZZAQ:v1p163/r1) supports this position. TAB-FULL (source note TAB-FULL:v7p45#2/r5) supports this position. TAB-FULL (source note TAB-FULL:v7p45#2/r6) supports this position. TAB-FULL (source note TAB-FULL:v7p48/r1) supports this position. TAB-FULL (source note TAB-FULL:v7p48/r2) supports this position. TAB-FULL (source note TAB-FULL:v7p48/r3) supports this position. TAB-FULL (source note TAB-FULL:v7p48/r4) supports this position. ZAJJAJ (source note ZAJJAJ:v2p54/r3) supports this position. ZAJJAJ (source note ZAJJAJ:v2p54/r4) supports this position. ZAJJAJ (source note ZAJJAJ:v2p54/r5) supports this position. ZAJJAJ (source note ZAJJAJ:v2p54/r6) supports this position. IBNABIHATIM (source note IBNABIHATIM:v3p958/r4) supports this position. WAHIDI-WAJIZ (source note WAHIDI-WAJIZ:4:43-44/r2) supports this position. IBNATIYYA-FULL (source note IBNATIYYA-FULL:v2p233/r9) supports this position. [25 notes: TABRISI(ابن عباس ومجاهد وقتادة); QURTUBI-FULL(عمر بن الخطاب); ABUHAYYAN-FULL(ابن عباس); IBNKATHIR-FULL; ITQAN(ابن عمر); ASAD-NOTES(some of the commentators); STUDYQURAN(minority tradition); ABDURRAZZAQ(قتادة); TAB-FULL(أبو رزين,أبو وائل، أبو رزين، إبراهيم,ابن عباس,قتادة,مجاهد); ZAJJAJ(أصحاب النبي,عمر بن الخطاب); IBNABIHATIM(ابن عباس); WAHIDI-WAJIZ; IBNATIYYA-FULL; QURTUBI-FULL(مجاهد وعكرمة وقتادة)+; RAZI-FULL+]
- 4:43/q07/p2 A staged or qualified change — IBNJAWZI-ZAD, d. 597 (author): This verse temporarily allowed drinking outside prayer times, then the later prohibition of wine abrogated that allowance. QURTUBI-FULL, d. 671 (أبو العلاء بن الشخير): Ibn al-Shikhkhīr says the Prophet's reports abrogated one another, just as Qur'anic verses do. QURTUBI-FULL, d. 671 (أبو إسحاق): Abū Isḥāq considers the report excusing ghusl after intercourse without emission abrogated. QURTUBI-FULL, d. 671 (الترمذي): Al-Tirmidhī says the no-ghusl ruling applied in early Islam and was later abrogated. IBNKATHIR-FULL, d. 774 (قتادة): Qatada says they avoided intoxication when prayer times arrived, and the final prohibition of wine later abrogated this rule. IBNASHUR-FULL, d. 1393 (أنس بن مالك وأبو عبيدة وابن المسيب والضحاك وعطاء ومجاهد ومسروق والنخعي وزيد بن أسلم وعمرو بن دينار وعكرمة وابن شهاب وقتادة): These authorities are reported to say the mosque-passage license belonged to an early period when many Emigrants and Helpers had doors opening into the mosque; it was later superseded after the doors were closed except Abū Bakr’s wicket, with reports also naming the Prophet and Abū Bakr, or ʿAlī, as exceptions. [6 notes: IBNJAWZI-ZAD; QURTUBI-FULL(أبو إسحاق,أبو العلاء بن الشخير,الترمذي); IBNKATHIR-FULL(قتادة); IBNASHUR-FULL(أنس بن مالك وأبو عبيدة وابن المسيب والضحاك وعطاء ومجاهد ومسروق والنخعي وزيد بن أسلم وعمرو بن دينار وعكرمة وابن شهاب وقتادة)]
- 4:43/q07/p3 The restriction remains operative — QURTUBI-FULL, d. 671 (عمر بن الخطاب): Al-Nahhas cites Umar recalling that a caller of the Prophet announced that no drunk person should approach prayer; on the sleepiness or urinary-need readings, al-Qurtubi says the verse is not abrogated. ABUHAYYAN-FULL, d. 745 (الجمهور): The majority regard this verse as still legally operative. ABUHAYYAN-FULL, d. 745 (author): Abū Ḥayyān calls ʿIkrima’s account objectionable: this verse neither licenses wine nor allows prayer while drunk, so it was not abrogated. ASAD-NOTES, d. 1412 (author): Asad rejects abrogation as untenable and says nothing warrants treating this continuing safeguard as redundant after the general intoxicant ban; he also rejects the abrogation doctrine in 2:106. | mentions 2:106, 5:90 [4 notes: QURTUBI-FULL(عمر بن الخطاب); ABUHAYYAN-FULL(الجمهور); ASAD-NOTES]
- 4:43/q07/p4 Abrogated by the ablution verse — QURTUBI-FULL, d. 671 (ابن عباس): Ibn Abbas says the prohibition on praying drunk was abrogated by 5:6: first they were told not to pray intoxicated, then to pray in every state; this preceded alcohol's final prohibition. | mentions 5:6 ABUHAYYAN-FULL, d. 745 (عكرمة): ʿIkrima says the prayer clause was abrogated by the ablution verse: first they could delay prayer until sober, then were commanded to pray in every state, before wine was prohibited. | mentions 5:6 [2 notes: QURTUBI-FULL(ابن عباس); ABUHAYYAN-FULL(عكرمة)]

## 4:43/q08 What legal acts of an intoxicated person take effect?  [وَأَنتُمْ سُكَٰرَىٰ · law]
turns on: The cited interpretations, reports, language arguments, and legal evidence differ.
- 4:43/q08/p1 a drunk person’s contracts, including marriage, sale, and purchase, are invalid; acts that terminate contracts are also ineffective except for the disputed case of divorce — TABRISI, d. 548 (author): A drunk person’s contracts, including marriage, sale, and purchase, are invalid; acts that terminate contracts are also ineffective except for the disputed case of divorce. IBNATIYYA-FULL (source note IBNATIYYA-FULL:v2p57/r1) supports this position. IBNATIYYA-FULL (source note IBNATIYYA-FULL:v2p57/r2) supports this position. IBNATIYYA-FULL (source note IBNATIYYA-FULL:v2p57/r3) supports this position. IBNATIYYA-FULL (source note IBNATIYYA-FULL:v2p57/r4) supports this position. IBNATIYYA-FULL (source note IBNATIYYA-FULL:v2p57/r5) supports this position. [6 notes: TABRISI; IBNATIYYA-FULL(النبي صلى الله عليه وسلم,عثمان بن عفان وغيره)]
- 4:43/q08/p2 abu hanifa holds that a drunk person’s divorce takes effect — TABRISI, d. 548 (أبو حنيفة): Abu Hanifa holds that a drunk person’s divorce takes effect. [1 notes: TABRISI(أبو حنيفة)]
- 4:43/q08/p3 al-shafiʿi holds in one of his two positions that a drunk person’s divorce does not take effect — TABRISI, d. 548 (الشافعي): Al-Shafiʿi holds in one of his two positions that a drunk person’s divorce does not take effect. [1 notes: TABRISI(الشافعي)]
- 4:43/q08/p4 in al-tabrisi’s school, a drunk person remains liable to all fixed penalties and retaliation, including amputation for theft and punishment for slander or adultery, based on general verses and the school’s consensus — TABRISI, d. 548 (author): In al-Tabrisi’s school, a drunk person remains liable to all fixed penalties and retaliation, including amputation for theft and punishment for slander or adultery, based on general verses and the school’s consensus. [1 notes: TABRISI]
- 4:43/q08/p5 ibn abd al-hakam says a drunkard's divorce and emancipation do not take effect — QURTUBI-FULL, d. 671 (محمد بن عبد الحكم): Ibn Abd al-Hakam says a drunkard's divorce and emancipation do not take effect. [1 notes: QURTUBI-FULL(محمد بن عبد الحكم)]
- 4:43/q08/p6 al-thawri defines intoxication as mental derangement; a person who confuses his recitation and speaks without knowing what he says is flogged — QURTUBI-FULL, d. 671 (سفيان الثوري): Al-Thawri defines intoxication as mental derangement; a person who confuses his recitation and speaks without knowing what he says is flogged. [1 notes: QURTUBI-FULL(سفيان الثوري)]
- 4:43/q08/p7 uthman held that a drunk person's divorce does not take effect because the drunk person does not know what he says — QURTUBI-FULL, d. 671 (عثمان بن عفان): Uthman held that a drunk person's divorce does not take effect because the drunk person does not know what he says. [1 notes: QURTUBI-FULL(عثمان بن عفان)]
- 4:43/q08/p8 these authorities hold that an intoxicated person's divorce is ineffective; al-tahawi adopts this position — QURTUBI-FULL, d. 671 (ابن عباس وطاوس وعطاء والقاسم وربيعة والليث وإسحاق وأبو ثور والمزني والطحاوي): These authorities hold that an intoxicated person's divorce is ineffective; al-Tahawi adopts this position. [1 notes: QURTUBI-FULL(ابن عباس وطاوس وعطاء والقاسم وربيعة والليث وإسحاق وأبو ثور والمزني والطحاوي)]
- 4:43/q08/p9 scholars agree a mentally impaired person's divorce is invalid, and the drunk is impaired like one afflicted by delusion; a person whose mind is lost through banj likewise has no effective divorce — QURTUBI-FULL, d. 671 (الطحاوي): Al-Tahawi argues that scholars agree a mentally impaired person's divorce is invalid, and the drunk is impaired like one afflicted by delusion; a person whose mind is lost through banj likewise has no effective divorce. [1 notes: QURTUBI-FULL(الطحاوي)]
- 4:43/q08/p10 another group allows the drunkard's divorce: this is reported from umar, muawiya, and successors, and is the position of abu hanifa, al-thawri, and al-awzai; al-shafi'i differed within his school — QURTUBI-FULL, d. 671 (عمر ومعاوية وجماعة من التابعين وأبو حنيفة والثوري والأوزاعي): Another group allows the drunkard's divorce: this is reported from Umar, Muawiya, and successors, and is the position of Abu Hanifa, al-Thawri, and al-Awzai; al-Shafi'i differed within his school. [1 notes: QURTUBI-FULL(عمر ومعاوية وجماعة من التابعين وأبو حنيفة والثوري والأوزاعي)]
- 4:43/q08/p11 malik counts divorce and retaliatory liability for wounds or killing as effective for a drunk, but not marriage or sale — QURTUBI-FULL, d. 671 (مالك): Malik counts divorce and retaliatory liability for wounds or killing as effective for a drunk, but not marriage or sale. [1 notes: QURTUBI-FULL(مالك)]
- 4:43/q08/p12 abu hanifa validates all a drunkard's acts and contracts as if sober, except that an apostasy does not dissolve his marriage as a discretionary exception — QURTUBI-FULL, d. 671 (أبو حنيفة): Abu Hanifa validates all a drunkard's acts and contracts as if sober, except that an apostasy does not dissolve his marriage as a discretionary exception. [1 notes: QURTUBI-FULL(أبو حنيفة)]
- 4:43/q08/p13 al-maziri says a rare report among malikis holds that the drunkard's divorce does not take effect — QURTUBI-FULL, d. 671 (المازري): Al-Maziri says a rare report among Malikis holds that the drunkard's divorce does not take effect. [1 notes: QURTUBI-FULL(المازري)]
- 4:43/q08/p14 the drunken person's mistaken recitation of words of disbelief is not apostasy: he was still addressed as a believer, and no separation or renewal of faith was ordered — NASAFI-FULL, d. 710 (author): The drunken person's mistaken recitation of words of disbelief is not apostasy: he was still addressed as a believer, and no separation or renewal of faith was ordered. [1 notes: NASAFI-FULL]
- 4:43/q08/p15 mālik binds a drunk person to divorce and injury-related retaliation or compensation, but not to marriage or sale — ABUHAYYAN-FULL, d. 745 (مالك): Mālik binds a drunk person to divorce and injury-related retaliation or compensation, but not to marriage or sale. [1 notes: ABUHAYYAN-FULL(مالك)]
- 4:43/q08/p16 al-māwardī notes a rare report in their school that a drunk person’s divorce is not binding — ABUHAYYAN-FULL, d. 745 (الماوردي): Al-Māwardī notes a rare report in their school that a drunk person’s divorce is not binding. [1 notes: ABUHAYYAN-FULL(الماوردي)]
- 4:43/q08/p17 muḥammad ibn ʿabd al-ḥakam says a drunk person’s divorce and emancipation are not binding — ABUHAYYAN-FULL, d. 745 (محمد بن عبد الحكم): Muḥammad ibn ʿAbd al-Ḥakam says a drunk person’s divorce and emancipation are not binding. [1 notes: ABUHAYYAN-FULL(محمد بن عبد الحكم)]
- 4:43/q08/p18 the verse indicates that an intoxicated person may not understand his words, underlying disagreement about the legal effect of his divorce — ABUHAYYAN-FULL, d. 745 (author): The verse indicates that an intoxicated person may not understand his words, underlying disagreement about the legal effect of his divorce. [1 notes: ABUHAYYAN-FULL]
- 4:43/q08/p19 ʿuthmān, ibn ʿabbās, and the named jurists hold that a drunk person’s divorce is not binding; al-ṭabarī prefers this — ABUHAYYAN-FULL, d. 745 (عثمان وابن عباس وطاووس وعطاء والقاسم وربيعة والليث وإسحاق وأبو ثور والمزني): ʿUthmān, Ibn ʿAbbās, and the named jurists hold that a drunk person’s divorce is not binding; al-Ṭabarī prefers this. [1 notes: ABUHAYYAN-FULL(عثمان وابن عباس وطاووس وعطاء والقاسم وربيعة والليث وإسحاق وأبو ثور والمزني)]
- 4:43/q08/p20 an imbecile’s divorce is invalid and the drunk is mentally impaired; loss of reason from anesthetic likewise invalidates divorce — ABUHAYYAN-FULL, d. 745 (الطبري): Al-Ṭabarī argues that an imbecile’s divorce is invalid and the drunk is mentally impaired; loss of reason from anesthetic likewise invalidates divorce. [1 notes: ABUHAYYAN-FULL(الطبري)+]
- 4:43/q08/p21 ʿumar, muʿāwiya, and a group of successors hold that the drunk person’s divorce takes effect; abū ḥanīfa, al-thawrī, and al-awzāʿī agree — ABUHAYYAN-FULL, d. 745 (عمر ومعاوية وجماعة من التابعين): ʿUmar, Muʿāwiya, and a group of Successors hold that the drunk person’s divorce takes effect; Abū Ḥanīfa, al-Thawrī, and al-Awzāʿī agree. [1 notes: ABUHAYYAN-FULL(عمر ومعاوية وجماعة من التابعين)]
- 4:43/q08/p22 abū ḥanīfa treats a drunk person’s acts and contracts like a sober person’s, except apostasy does not separate him from his wife — ABUHAYYAN-FULL, d. 745 (أبو حنيفة): Abū Ḥanīfa treats a drunk person’s acts and contracts like a sober person’s, except apostasy does not separate him from his wife. [1 notes: ABUHAYYAN-FULL(أبو حنيفة)]
- 4:43/q08/p23 abū yūsuf and al-shāfiʿī say a drunk person is an apostate while intoxicated; al-shāfiʿī does not execute or seek repentance then, and his position on divorce varied — ABUHAYYAN-FULL, d. 745 (أبو يوسف والشافعي): Abū Yūsuf and al-Shāfiʿī say a drunk person is an apostate while intoxicated; al-Shāfiʿī does not execute or seek repentance then, and his position on divorce varied. [1 notes: ABUHAYYAN-FULL(أبو يوسف والشافعي)]
- 4:43/q08/p24 scholars agree that a drunk person’s sale and purchase are invalid, but hold him liable for damage, homicide, and penalties; hanafis count divorce and manumission as effective punishment, unlike al-shafi‘i — BURSEVI, d. 1127 (author): Scholars agree that a drunk person’s sale and purchase are invalid, but hold him liable for damage, homicide, and penalties; Hanafis count divorce and manumission as effective punishment, unlike al-Shafi‘i. [1 notes: BURSEVI]
- 4:43/q08/p25 al-shihab says intoxication caused by one's own act does not remove the duty to pray; an intoxicated person's divorce and similar acts take effect, and a prayer time wholly spent drunk must be made up — ALUSI-FULL, d. 1270 (الشهاب): Al-Shihab says intoxication caused by one's own act does not remove the duty to pray; an intoxicated person's divorce and similar acts take effect, and a prayer time wholly spent drunk must be made up. [1 notes: ALUSI-FULL(الشهاب)]

## 4:43/q09 How is junub formed and used?  [جُنُبًا · meaning]
turns on: The cited interpretations, reports, language arguments, and legal evidence differ.
- 4:43/q09/p1 One form applies across genders and numbers — TABRISI, d. 548 (author): Junuban is coordinated with the preceding circumstantial phrase and means a plural group; abiri sabil is an accusative exception. TABRISI, d. 548 (author): Junub applies to male or female, singular or plural, describing a person in janaba. IBNJAWZI-ZAD, d. 597 (الزجاج): Al-Zajjāj notes that junub has the same form for one man, two men, and a group, as raḍī does. QURTUBI-FULL, d. 671 (أبو حنيفة): Abu Hanifa reads the junub traveler passing through as a traveler without water, who performs dry ablution and prays. QURTUBI-FULL, d. 671 (author): The forms تجنبتم, أجنبتم, and جنبتم all convey becoming junub; junub is generally indeclinable for gender, number, and plurality as a verbal noun-like form, though variants exist. QURTUBI-FULL, d. 671 (author): Some Arabic usage pluralizes junub as أجناب, like عنق/أعناق and طنب/أطناب; those who use جانب for the singular say جناب in the plural, like راكب/ركاب. QURTUBI-FULL, d. 671 (أحمد وإسحاق): Aḥmad and Isḥāq allow a junub to sit in the mosque after performing ablution, according to Ibn al-Mundhir. QURTUBI-FULL, d. 671 (أصحاب الرأي): The adherents of raʾy allow a traveling junub to perform tayammum, enter a mosque to draw water from its spring, and leave with it. QURTUBI-FULL, d. 671 (الثوري وإسحاق بن راهويه): Al-Thawrī and Isḥāq ibn Rāhwayh say a junub may cross a mosque only when necessary, after performing tayammum. QURTUBI-FULL, d. 671 (عطاء بن يسار): ʿAṭāʾ ibn Yasār reports that some Companions who became junub performed ablution, then came to the mosque and conversed there. BAYDAWI-FULL, d. 685 (author): Junuban coordinates with the circumstantial clause “while you are intoxicated”; janub applies to all genders and numbers like a verbal noun. NASAFI-FULL, d. 710 (author): Junub has the same form for singular and plural and for masculine and feminine because it functions like the verbal noun ijnāb. ABUHAYYAN-FULL, d. 745 (الزمخشري): Al-Zamakhsharī says junub has one form for singular and plural and for masculine and feminine, like the verbal noun ijnaab; Abū Ḥayyān calls it fluent, Qurʾānic usage and notes plural forms جنبون، أجناب and dual جنبان. ABUHAYYAN-FULL, d. 745 (أحمد وإسحاق): Aḥmad and Isḥāq allow a junub who has performed ablution to sit in the mosque. IBNKATHIR-FULL, d. 774 (علي): A second interpretation attributed to Ali takes the exception to mean a traveler who becomes junub and lacks water may perform prayer until water is available; reports from Ibn Abbas in one version, Sa'id ibn Jubayr, and others say likewise. IBNKATHIR-FULL, d. 774 (أبو حنيفة ومالك والشافعي): Abu Hanifa, Malik, and al-Shafi‘i hold that a junub person may not remain in a mosque until bathing, or performing tayammum if water is unavailable or unusable. IBNKATHIR-FULL, d. 774 (أحمد): Ahmad permits a junub person to remain in the mosque after performing wudu. IBNKATHIR-FULL, d. 774 (عطاء بن يسار): Ataa ibn Yasar saw Companions sitting in the mosque while junub after performing prayer wudu; Ibn Kathir calls this report sound on Muslim’s criteria. BURHAN, d. 794 (author): Singular forms also occur in descriptions of groups, including junub, ẓahīr, najiyy, and the generic singular al-ṭifl. DURR-FULL, d. 911 (مجاهد): Mujahid says a junub person or menstruant may not pass through a mosque; the verse instead permits the traveler who lacks water to perform tayammum and pray. DURR-FULL, d. 911 (ابن عباس): Ibn Abbas says a person with smallpox, wounds, or ulcers who becomes junub and fears dying from bathing may perform tayammum. ELMALILI, d. 1361 (author): Junub denotes a person in janaba after emission; it can be used as a singular noun or a plural. IBNASHUR-FULL, d. 1393 (author): “Wa-lā junuban” is coordinated with the circumstantial clause “while you are intoxicated”; its accusative form confirms that the circumstantial clause occupies an accusative position. IBNASHUR-FULL, d. 1393 (author): As a verbal-noun adjective, junub does not take a plural form even though it describes a plural subject. AKHFASH (source note AKHFASH:v1p258/r3) supports this position. THALABI (source note THALABI:v3p312/r7) supports this position. THALABI (source note THALABI:v3p312/r8) supports this position. THALABI (source note THALABI:v3p312/r9) supports this position. JISHUMI (source note JISHUMI:v2p1565/r7) supports this position. BAGHAWI-FULL (source note BAGHAWI-FULL:v2p219#2/r1) supports this position. BAGHAWI-FULL (source note BAGHAWI-FULL:v2p219#2/r2) supports this position. [31 notes: TABRISI; IBNJAWZI-ZAD(الزجاج); QURTUBI-FULL(أبو حنيفة,أحمد وإسحاق,أصحاب الرأي,الثوري وإسحاق بن راهويه,عطاء بن يسار); BAYDAWI-FULL; NASAFI-FULL; ABUHAYYAN-FULL(أحمد وإسحاق,الزمخشري); IBNKATHIR-FULL(أبو حنيفة ومالك والشافعي,أحمد,عطاء بن يسار,علي); BURHAN; DURR-FULL(ابن عباس,مجاهد); ELMALILI; IBNASHUR-FULL; AKHFASH; THALABI(إبراهيم النخعي); JISHUMI; BAGHAWI-FULL]
- 4:43/q09/p2 Derived from distance — IBNJAWZI-ZAD, d. 597 (ابن قتيبة): Ibn Qutayba says junub derives from distance or separation. QURTUBI-FULL, d. 671 (author): The root sense is distance: major emission leaves the person distant from the state required for prayer. ALUSI-FULL, d. 1270 (author): Junub is invariant in the eloquent language across gender and number, though some Arabs inflect it; Abu al-Baqa derives it from separation. [3 notes: IBNJAWZI-ZAD(ابن قتيبة); QURTUBI-FULL; ALUSI-FULL]

## 4:43/q10 What makes someone junub?  [جُنُبًا · law]
turns on: The cited interpretations, reports, language arguments, and legal evidence differ.
- 4:43/q10/p1 Emission or penetration makes one junub — QURTUBI-FULL, d. 671 (عائشة): ʿĀʾisha reports the Prophet ruled that ghusl is obligatory when the circumcised parts touch after intercourse. QURTUBI-FULL, d. 671 (أبو هريرة): Abū Hurayra reports the Prophet made ghusl obligatory after intercourse, with Muslim adding that this applies even without emission. QURTUBI-FULL, d. 671 (ابن القصار): Ibn al-Qaṣṣār says later generations of scholars agreed to follow the report about the two circumcised parts, ending the earlier disagreement. QURTUBI-FULL, d. 671 (الجمهور من الأمة): The majority define a junub as one impure through emission or penetration of the circumcised parts. QURTUBI-FULL, d. 671 (أبي بن كعب): Ubayy reports that the Prophet instructed a man who had intercourse without emission to wash the affected area, perform ablution, and pray. QURTUBI-FULL, d. 671 (author): The author aligns with the scholars of the community: ghusl becomes obligatory upon penetration of the circumcised parts, whether or not emission occurs. BIQAI-FULL, d. 885 (author): Junub refers to emission of semen, actual or near at hand through the meeting of the circumcised parts; it applies whether semen results from intercourse or otherwise. ELMALILI, d. 1361 (author): The wording is agreed to signify intercourse, specifically the meeting of the circumcised parts, which requires ghusl. IBNASHUR-FULL, d. 1393 (author): The endpoint forbids prayer while junub until washing; since prayer is never waived, the verse establishes ghusl as obligatory. BAGHAWI-FULL (source note BAGHAWI-FULL:v2p221/r1) supports this position. BAGHAWI-FULL (source note BAGHAWI-FULL:v2p221/r2) supports this position. IBNATIYYA-FULL (source note IBNATIYYA-FULL:v2p57/r10) supports this position. IBNATIYYA-FULL (source note IBNATIYYA-FULL:v2p57/r11) supports this position. IBNATIYYA-FULL (source note IBNATIYYA-FULL:v2p57/r12) supports this position. IBNATIYYA-FULL (source note IBNATIYYA-FULL:v2p57/r13) supports this position. [15 notes: QURTUBI-FULL(أبو هريرة,أبي بن كعب,ابن القصار,الجمهور من الأمة,عائشة); BIQAI-FULL; ELMALILI; IBNASHUR-FULL; BAGHAWI-FULL(عائشة); IBNATIYYA-FULL(بعض الصحابة,جمهور الأمة,فرقة)]
- 4:43/q10/p2 Ghusl is due only after emission — QURTUBI-FULL, d. 671 (بعض الصحابة): Some Companions held that ghusl is required only after emission, citing the report that water is due from water. [1 notes: QURTUBI-FULL(بعض الصحابة)]

## 4:43/q11 What does washing after janābah require?  [حَتَّىٰ تَغْتَسِلُوا۟ · law]
turns on: The cited interpretations, reports, language arguments, and legal evidence differ.
- 4:43/q11/p1 the maliki school differs over whether moving a ring and rubbing between the fingers are obligatory — IBNATIYYA-FULL, d. 542 (أهل المذهب): The Maliki school differs over whether moving a ring and rubbing between the fingers are obligatory. THALABI (source note THALABI:v3p319/r6) supports this position. THALABI (source note THALABI:v3p319/r7) supports this position. THALABI (source note THALABI:v3p319/r8) supports this position. THALABI (source note THALABI:v3p319/r9) supports this position. WAHIDI-WAJIZ (source note WAHIDI-WAJIZ:4:43-44/r5) supports this position. [6 notes: IBNATIYYA-FULL(أهل المذهب); THALABI(المزني,علي); WAHIDI-WAJIZ]
- 4:43/q11/p2 abū al-faraj cites a report that water must reach beneath every hair and the skin be thoroughly cleaned, which he takes to support rubbing — QURTUBI-FULL, d. 671 (أبو الفرج عمرو بن محمد المالكي): Abū al-Faraj cites a report that water must reach beneath every hair and the skin be thoroughly cleaned, which he takes to support rubbing. [1 notes: QURTUBI-FULL(أبو الفرج عمرو بن محمد المالكي)]
- 4:43/q11/p3 abū ʿumar argues that arabic can call either rubbing with water or pouring water a washing; ablution and ghusl may therefore be independent forms, not analogized to each other — QURTUBI-FULL, d. 671 (أبو عمر): Abū ʿUmar argues that Arabic can call either rubbing with water or pouring water a washing; ablution and ghusl may therefore be independent forms, not analogized to each other. [1 notes: QURTUBI-FULL(أبو عمر)]
- 4:43/q11/p4 sufyān ibn ʿuyayna instead interprets cleaning the skin as washing and cleaning the genitals, not rubbing the whole body — QURTUBI-FULL, d. 671 (سفيان بن عيينة): Sufyān ibn ʿUyayna instead interprets cleaning the skin as washing and cleaning the genitals, not rubbing the whole body. [1 notes: QURTUBI-FULL(سفيان بن عيينة)]
- 4:43/q11/p5 muslim records from ʿāʾisha and umm qays that the prophet poured water on a child after it urinated without rubbing, an analogy supporting sufficiency of pouring — QURTUBI-FULL, d. 671 (عائشة وأم قيس بنت محصن): Muslim records from ʿĀʾisha and Umm Qays that the Prophet poured water on a child after it urinated without rubbing, an analogy supporting sufficiency of pouring. [1 notes: QURTUBI-FULL(عائشة وأم قيس بنت محصن)]
- 4:43/q11/p6 the majority hold that complete water coverage by pouring or immersion suffices for ghusl, without rubbing, based on reports describing the prophet's bath — QURTUBI-FULL, d. 671 (الجمهور من العلماء): The majority hold that complete water coverage by pouring or immersion suffices for ghusl, without rubbing, based on reports describing the Prophet's bath. [1 notes: QURTUBI-FULL(الجمهور من العلماء)]
- 4:43/q11/p7 ibn al-ʿarabī criticizes abū al-faraj, saying mālik never expressly or by inference held that ghusl without rubbing sufficed and that this was abū al-faraj's error — QURTUBI-FULL, d. 671 (ابن العربي): Ibn al-ʿArabī criticizes Abū al-Faraj, saying Mālik never expressly or by inference held that ghusl without rubbing sufficed and that this was Abū al-Faraj's error. [1 notes: QURTUBI-FULL(ابن العربي)]
- 4:43/q11/p8 abū ʿumar says the mālik report explicitly validates immersion without rubbing or ablution, while the well-known mālikī position requires rubbing by analogy with ablution — QURTUBI-FULL, d. 671 (أبو عمر): Abū ʿUmar says the Mālik report explicitly validates immersion without rubbing or ablution, while the well-known Mālikī position requires rubbing by analogy with ablution. [1 notes: QURTUBI-FULL(أبو عمر)]
- 4:43/q11/p9 mālik, al-ṭabarī, al-layth, al-awzāʿī, and others do not require mouth or nose rinsing in ghusl or ablution because these are internal parts — QURTUBI-FULL, d. 671 (مالك): Mālik, al-Ṭabarī, al-Layth, al-Awzāʿī, and others do not require mouth or nose rinsing in ghusl or ablution because these are internal parts. [1 notes: QURTUBI-FULL(مالك)]
- 4:43/q11/p10 ibn abī laylā, ḥammād ibn abī sulaymān, isḥāq, aḥmad, some of dāwūd's companions, and reports from al-zuhrī and ʿaṭāʾ require both rinses in both ghusl and ablution — QURTUBI-FULL, d. 671 (ابن أبي ليلى وحماد بن أبي سليمان): Ibn Abī Laylā, Ḥammād ibn Abī Sulaymān, Isḥāq, Aḥmad, some of Dāwūd's companions, and reports from al-Zuhrī and ʿAṭāʾ require both rinses in both ghusl and ablution. - 4:43/q11/p11 another report from aḥmad makes mouth rinsing recommended but nose rinsing obligatory, a position also held by some of dāwūd's companions — QURTUBI-FULL, d. 671 (أحمد وبعض أصحاب داود): Another report from Aḥmad makes mouth rinsing recommended but nose rinsing obligatory, a position also held by some of Dāwūd's companions. [1 notes: QURTUBI-FULL(أحمد وبعض أصحاب داود)]
- 4:43/q11/p12 a report from ibn ʿumar says prayer was reduced from fifty to five, ghusl from seven washes to one, and washing urine from clothing likewise to one; ibn ʿabd al-barr calls its chain weak and — QURTUBI-FULL, d. 671 (ابن عمر): A report from Ibn ʿUmar says prayer was reduced from fifty to five, ghusl from seven washes to one, and washing urine from clothing likewise to one; Ibn ʿAbd al-Barr calls its chain weak and notes Shuʿba is not strong. [1 notes: QURTUBI-FULL(ابن عمر)]
- 4:43/q11/p13 ibn al-qāsim transmits mālik's position that a junub need not work water through the beard — QURTUBI-FULL, d. 671 (ابن القاسم): Ibn al-Qāsim transmits Mālik's position that a junub need not work water through the beard. [1 notes: QURTUBI-FULL(ابن القاسم)]
- 4:43/q11/p14 ashhab transmits the contrary position from mālik: a junub must work water through the beard — QURTUBI-FULL, d. 671 (أشهب): Ashhab transmits the contrary position from Mālik: a junub must work water through the beard. [1 notes: QURTUBI-FULL(أشهب)]
- 4:43/q11/p15 ibn ʿabd al-ḥakam prefers working water through the beard because the prophet worked water through his hair; the author reasons that the skin beneath the beard is part of the body to be washed — QURTUBI-FULL, d. 671 (ابن عبد الحكم): Ibn ʿAbd al-Ḥakam prefers working water through the beard because the Prophet worked water through his hair; the author reasons that the skin beneath the beard is part of the body to be washed. [1 notes: QURTUBI-FULL(ابن عبد الحكم)+]
- 4:43/q11/p16 the author says lighter ablution rules, such as wiping over footwear, do not transfer to ghusl, which requires water to reach the entire body — QURTUBI-FULL, d. 671 (author): The author says lighter ablution rules, such as wiping over footwear, do not transfer to ghusl, which requires water to reach the entire body. [1 notes: QURTUBI-FULL]
- 4:43/q11/p17 abū ḥanīfa and others require rinsing the mouth and nose in ghusl because both are part of the face; omission requires repeating prayer, though omission in ablution does not — QURTUBI-FULL, d. 671 (أبو حنيفة): Abū Ḥanīfa and others require rinsing the mouth and nose in ghusl because both are part of the face; omission requires repeating prayer, though omission in ablution does not. - 4:43/q11/p18 anas reports the prophet used one mudd for ablution and one ṣāʿ to five mudds for ghusl; another version gives five makākīk for ghusl and one for ablution — QURTUBI-FULL, d. 671 (أنس): Anas reports the Prophet used one mudd for ablution and one ṣāʿ to five mudds for ghusl; another version gives five makākīk for ghusl and one for ablution. - 4:43/q11/p19 those who require the rinses cite this verse and the command to wash faces, adding that the prophet never omitted them in ablution or ghusl — QURTUBI-FULL, d. 671 (من أوجبهما): Those who require the rinses cite this verse and the command to wash faces, adding that the Prophet never omitted them in ablution or ghusl. | mentions 5:6 [1 notes: QURTUBI-FULL(من أوجبهما)]
- 4:43/q11/p20 those who distinguish the two say the prophet performed mouth rinsing without commanding it, but both performed and commanded nose rinsing, whose command signifies obligation — QURTUBI-FULL, d. 671 (من فرق بينهما): Those who distinguish the two say the Prophet performed mouth rinsing without commanding it, but both performed and commanded nose rinsing, whose command signifies obligation. - 4:43/q11/p21 the author's scholars require intention for ghusl, as do mālik, al-shāfiʿī, aḥmad, isḥāq, and abū thawr; they also require it for ablution and tayammum — QURTUBI-FULL, d. 671 (علماؤنا): The author's scholars require intention for ghusl, as do Mālik, al-Shāfiʿī, Aḥmad, Isḥāq, and Abū Thawr; they also require it for ablution and tayammum. - 4:43/q11/p23 ibn ʿabbas says a wounded, sore-covered, or smallpox patient in janaba who fears dying from bathing may perform tayammum — QURTUBI-FULL, d. 671 (ابن عباس): Ibn ʿAbbas says a wounded, sore-covered, or smallpox patient in janaba who fears dying from bathing may perform tayammum. - 4:43/q11/p24 in the cold dhat al-salasil campaign, ʿamr b. al-ʿas feared bathing would kill him, used tayammum, and led dawn prayer while junub; the prophet laughed after hearing his reason — QURTUBI-FULL, d. 671 (عمرو بن العاص): In the cold Dhat al-Salasil campaign, ʿAmr b. al-ʿAs feared bathing would kill him, used tayammum, and led dawn prayer while junub; the Prophet laughed after hearing his reason. - 4:43/q11/p26 one view limits mulamasa to hand-touch and says a junub person is not covered by the illness clause, so he must bathe or wait for water; it is reported from ʿumar and ibn masʿud — QURTUBI-FULL, d. 671 (عمر وابن مسعود): One view limits mulamasa to hand-touch and says a junub person is not covered by the illness clause, so he must bathe or wait for water; it is reported from ʿUmar and Ibn Masʿud. - 4:43/q11/p27 an opponent argues by consensus that forced intercourse without pleasure still obligates ghusl, so any touching or kissing should break ablution regardless of desire — QURTUBI-FULL, d. 671 (الخصم): An opponent argues by consensus that forced intercourse without pleasure still obligates ghusl, so any touching or kissing should break ablution regardless of desire. - 4:43/q11/p28 scholars agree that only nabidh is even discussed as a substitute drink for water in ablution or ghusl, but the verse's command to tayammum rules that substitution out — QURTUBI-FULL, d. 671 (author): Scholars agree that only nabidh is even discussed as a substitute drink for water in ablution or ghusl, but the verse's command to tayammum rules that substitution out. - 4:43/q11/p29 abu salama reportedly says a junub person who finds water after tayammum remains purified and needs neither ghusl nor ablution until a new occurrence — QURTUBI-FULL, d. 671 (أبو سلمة بن عبد الرحمن): Abu Salama reportedly says a junub person who finds water after tayammum remains purified and needs neither ghusl nor ablution until a new occurrence. - 4:43/q11/p30 the majority hold that a completed prayer after diligent water-search remains valid even if water is then found; some recommend repeating within the time after ablution or ghusl — QURTUBI-FULL, d. 671 (الجمهور): The majority hold that a completed prayer after diligent water-search remains valid even if water is then found; some recommend repeating within the time after ablution or ghusl. - 4:43/q11/p31 qāḍī ʿiyāḍ knows of no later adherents to the no-ghusl view except al-aʿmash and, afterward, dāwūd al-iṣfahānī — QURTUBI-FULL, d. 671 (القاضي عياض): Qāḍī ʿIyāḍ knows of no later adherents to the no-ghusl view except al-Aʿmash and, afterward, Dāwūd al-Iṣfahānī. ## 4:43/q12 Who are the passers-through excepted here?  [إِلَّا عَابِرِى سَبِيلٍ · referent]
turns on: The cited interpretations, reports, language arguments, and legal evidence differ.
- 4:43/q12/p1 Travelers lacking water — QURTUBI-FULL, d. 671 (author): ʿĀbir al-sabīl means a passerby or traveler on the road. QURTUBI-FULL, d. 671 (علي وابن عباس وابن جبير ومجاهد والحكم): ʿAlī, Ibn ʿAbbās, Ibn Jubayr, Mujāhid, and al-Ḥakam interpret the exception as referring to a traveler. NASAFI-FULL, d. 710 (author): The exception describes a junub traveler passing through, and the reference to travel reflects the usual lack of water on a journey. ABUHAYYAN-FULL, d. 745 (author): The traveler is called a passer of the road because he is on the road, as in the expression son of the road. SAMIN-DURR, d. 756 (غير مسمى): Alternatively, عابري describes جنبا, with إلا meaning غير: the prohibition concerns resident, unexcused people in janāba; this is clear if عبور means travel. DURR-FULL, d. 911 (علي بن أبي طالب): Ali says the exception concerns a traveler who becomes sexually impure, lacks water, performs tayammum, and prays until water becomes available. DURR-FULL, d. 911 (ابن عباس): Ibn Abbas identifies the exception as a traveler who has no water and therefore performs tayammum and prays. DURR-FULL, d. 911 (مجاهد): Mujahid glosses the exception as travelers who cannot find water. IBNASHUR-FULL, d. 1393 (author): Among scholars who use legal implications, the unqualified traveler exception is further limited by “if you find no water”; the general prohibition on junub people is otherwise narrowed by the later illness-or-travel condition. IBNASHUR-FULL, d. 1393 (author): If Muslims already understood that prayer may occur without purification, the traveler exception awaits clarification and the following conditions clarify it; otherwise, those conditions introduce tayammum rules anew. KURANYOLU-TEFSIR (Ali, İbn Abbas, İbn Cübeyr, Mücâhid, Ebû Hanîfe): Ali, Ibn Abbas, Ibn Jubayr, Mujahid, and Abu Hanifa understand passers as travelers, the translation's choice; travel can make water unavailable or too necessary to use, so tayammum is allowed for a junub traveler. MUJAHID (source note MUJAHID:v1p158/r2) supports this position. MUQATIL (source note MUQATIL:4:43/r4) supports this position. FARRA (source note FARRA:v1p268/r11) supports this position. FARRA (source note FARRA:v1p268/r12) supports this position. TAB-FULL (source note TAB-FULL:v7p50/r1) supports this position. TAB-FULL (source note TAB-FULL:v7p50/r2) supports this position. - 4:43/q12/p2 People crossing a mosque — TABRISI, d. 548 (عبد الله وسعيد بن المسيب والضحاك وعكرمة والحسن): Another interpretation is do not approach mosques for prayer or otherwise; the exception for passing supports this because crossing is physical movement through a place. TABRISI, d. 548 (author): Abir means crossing from one side of a river or road to the other. QURTUBI-FULL, d. 671 (author): The verb ʿabara describes crossing a road or river from one side to the other. QURTUBI-FULL, d. 671 (author): The author answers the comparison with minor impurity by saying it occurs often and cleansing it would cause hardship; the verse's crossing exception suffices. ABUHAYYAN-FULL, d. 745 (author): ʿĀbir means crossing or passing through; the cited verse uses عبر for traversing the heat of midday. ABUHAYYAN-FULL, d. 745 (الشافعي): Al-Shāfiʿī understands the passer as someone who walks through a mosque without staying there. ABUHAYYAN-FULL, d. 745 (الزمخشري): On the reading of prayer as the mosque, al-Zamakhsharī takes the exception to permit only passing through on a route to water, where water is in the mosque, or after a wet dream there. ABUHAYYAN-FULL, d. 745 (علي وابن عباس وابن جبير ومجاهد والحكم): These authorities identify the passer as a traveler: a junub may not approach prayer until washing, except a traveler may use tayammum. Abū Ḥanīfa and his companions, Mālik, al-Thawrī, and others bar even mosque passage. SAMIN-DURR, d. 756 (author): One parse makes عابري a circumstantial exception governed by the prohibition: do not approach in janāba except while traveling or crossing a mosque, depending on the interpretation. SAMIN-DURR, d. 756 (الزمخشري): Al-Zamakhshari takes the exception as a circumstantial phrase from the addressees generally, with travel as an additional excuse and crossing the path as its expression. SAMIN-DURR, d. 756 (غير مسمى): One interpretation supplies an omitted مضاف: ‘the places of prayer,’ meaning mosques; the exception for crossing a path supports this in one reading. IBNKATHIR-FULL, d. 774 (ابن عباس): Ibn Abbas interprets the exception as allowing a junub person to pass through a mosque without sitting; Ibn Kathir lists many authorities who agree, including Ibn Masud, Anas, Abu Ubayda, Sa'id ibn al-Musayyab, Mujahid, Qatada, and others. IBNKATHIR-FULL, d. 774 (ابن جرير): Ibn Jarir explains an abir al-sabil as one who passes and crosses through; Arabic uses the same root for crossing a road or river and calls a camel able to cross journeys abir asfar. DURR-FULL, d. 911 (ابن مسعود): Ibn Masud interprets the exception as passing through the mosque; he elsewhere permits a junub person to cross it in transit. ALUSI-FULL, d. 1270 (الشافعي): On the mosque interpretation of prayer, crossing through a mosque is allowed for a junub person; al-Shafi'i holds this, while the well-known Hanafi position forbids mosque entry absolutely. ALUSI-FULL, d. 1270 (من حمل الصلاة على مواضعها): One parsing takes prayer as its locations and crossing as passing through them, so the later condition states a distinct rule. ALUSI-FULL, d. 1270 (author): The recommended pause after “until you wash” and the spatial language of approaching, crossing, and travel support that interpretation. ALUSI-FULL, d. 1270 (الكثير): Many commentators take the passage otherwise than the spatial interpretation of prayer and crossing. IBNASHUR-FULL, d. 1393 (author): In Arabic, a traveler while on a journey is an “ʿābir al-sabīl,” from crossing or passing through a river or road. TABATABAI, d. 1402 (author): Al-ṣalāt here means the mosque, because the exception for passers-through fits a rule about a junub entering the mosque. STUDYQURAN (author): The exception covers junub travelers seeking temporary shelter in a mosque and Companions whose adjacent homes required crossing the Prophet’s mosque to leave. ABDURRAZZAQ (source note ABDURRAZZAQ:v1p163/r3) supports this position. IBNQUTAYBA-GHARIB (source note IBNQUTAYBA-GHARIB:v1p127#3/r1) supports this position. TAB-FULL (source note TAB-FULL:v7p59/r1) supports this position. MAWARDI-FULL (source note MAWARDI-FULL:v1p489/r8) supports this position. BAGHAWI-FULL (source note BAGHAWI-FULL:v2p219#2/r4) supports this position. BAGHAWI-FULL (source note BAGHAWI-FULL:v2p219#2/r5) supports this position. [27 notes: TABRISI(عبد الله وسعيد بن المسيب والضحاك وعكرمة والحسن); QURTUBI-FULL; ABUHAYYAN-FULL(الزمخشري,الشافعي,علي وابن عباس وابن جبير ومجاهد والحكم); SAMIN-DURR(الزمخشري,غير مسمى); IBNKATHIR-FULL(ابن جرير,ابن عباس); DURR-FULL(ابن مسعود); ALUSI-FULL(الشافعي,الكثير,من حمل الصلاة على مواضعها); IBNASHUR-FULL; TABATABAI; STUDYQURAN; ABDURRAZZAQ(عبد الله بن مسعود); IBNQUTAYBA-GHARIB; TAB-FULL; MAWARDI-FULL(ابن عباس في رواية الضحاك وابن يسار، جابر، الحسن، الزهري، النخعي); BAGHAWI-FULL(عبد الله بن مسعود وسعيد بن المسيب والضحاك والحسن وعكرمة والنخعي والزهري)]

## 4:43/q13 May a junub person enter, cross, or remain in a mosque?  [إِلَّا عَابِرِى سَبِيلٍ · law]
turns on: The cited interpretations, reports, language arguments, and legal evidence differ.

- 4:43/q13/p2 another interpretation bars a junub person from entering or sitting in mosques except to pass through; ibn ʿabbās and saʿīd ibn jubayr are reported with both views — IBNJAWZI-ZAD, d. 597 (ابن مسعود، أنس بن مالك، الحسن، سعيد بن المسيب، عكرمة، عطاء الخراساني، الزهري، عمرو بن دينار، أبو الضحى، أحمد، الشافعي، ابن قتيبة): Another interpretation bars a junub person from entering or sitting in mosques except to pass through; Ibn ʿAbbās and Saʿīd ibn Jubayr are reported with both views. - 4:43/q13/p3 some reports explain the verse by saying ansar homes opened onto the mosque, so junub residents had to cross it — QURTUBI-FULL, d. 671 (بعضهم): Some reports explain the verse by saying Ansar homes opened onto the mosque, so junub residents had to cross it. [1 notes: QURTUBI-FULL(بعضهم)]
- 4:43/q13/p4 ʿāʾisha reports that the prophet ordered houses opening onto the mosque to be redirected, explaining that he permits neither a menstruating woman nor a junub to remain in it — QURTUBI-FULL, d. 671 (عائشة): ʿĀʾisha reports that the Prophet ordered houses opening onto the mosque to be redirected, explaining that he permits neither a menstruating woman nor a junub to remain in it. [1 notes: QURTUBI-FULL(عائشة)]
- 4:43/q13/p5 a group permits a junub to enter a mosque, citing the prophet's statement that a believer is not impure; ibn al-mundhir endorses this position — QURTUBI-FULL, d. 671 (ابن المنذر): A group permits a junub to enter a mosque, citing the Prophet's statement that a believer is not impure; Ibn al-Mundhir endorses this position.
