- 4:43/q13/p6 a report says the prophet allowed no one to pass through or sit in the mosque except ʿalī — QURTUBI-FULL, d. 671 (النبي): A report says the Prophet allowed no one to pass through or sit in the mosque except ʿAlī. [1 notes: QURTUBI-FULL(النبي)]
- 4:43/q13/p7 abū saʿīd al-khudrī reports the prophet said that no muslim should become junub in the mosque except the prophet and ʿalī — QURTUBI-FULL, d. 671 (أبو سعيد الخدري): Abū Saʿīd al-Khudrī reports the Prophet said that no Muslim should become junub in the mosque except the Prophet and ʿAlī. [1 notes: QURTUBI-FULL(أبو سعيد الخدري)]
- 4:43/q13/p8 the scholars suggest the prophet's and ʿalī's homes were within or attached to the mosque, with their doors opening into it, explaining their passage while junub — QURTUBI-FULL, d. 671 (علماؤنا): The scholars suggest the Prophet's and ʿAlī's homes were within or attached to the mosque, with their doors opening into it, explaining their passage while junub. [1 notes: QURTUBI-FULL(علماؤنا)]
- 4:43/q13/p9 against the permissive report, the author argues that ablution does not remove major impurity and that a junub should not enter a mosque set apart for worship — QURTUBI-FULL, d. 671 (author): Against the permissive report, the author argues that ablution does not remove major impurity and that a junub should not enter a mosque set apart for worship. [1 notes: QURTUBI-FULL]
- 4:43/q13/p10 if a junub may not remain in a mosque, the author argues a fortiori that he may neither touch the muṣḥaf nor recite there — QURTUBI-FULL, d. 671 (author): If a junub may not remain in a mosque, the author argues a fortiori that he may neither touch the muṣḥaf nor recite there. [1 notes: QURTUBI-FULL]
- 4:43/q13/p11 abū ḥanīfa bars a junub person from passing through a mosque unless it contains water or is the route — BAYDAWI-FULL, d. 685 (أبو حنيفة): Abū Ḥanīfa bars a junub person from passing through a mosque unless it contains water or is the route. [1 notes: BAYDAWI-FULL(أبو حنيفة)]
- 4:43/q13/p12 it is said some anṣār had doors opening into the mosque and, after becoming junub, had no route except through it, so they received a concession — ABUHAYYAN-FULL, d. 745 (الأنصار): It is said some Anṣār had doors opening into the mosque and, after becoming junub, had no route except through it, so they received a concession. [1 notes: ABUHAYYAN-FULL(الأنصار)]
- 4:43/q13/p13 a report says the prophet forbade anyone to sit or pass through the mosque while junub except ʿalī, whose house was inside it — ABUHAYYAN-FULL, d. 745 (رسول الله صلى الله عليه وسلم): A report says the Prophet forbade anyone to sit or pass through the mosque while junub except ʿAlī, whose house was inside it. [1 notes: ABUHAYYAN-FULL(رسول الله صلى الله عليه وسلم)]
- 4:43/q13/p14 al-layth permits passing through only if the junub person's own door opens onto the mosque — ABUHAYYAN-FULL, d. 745 (الليث): Al-Layth permits passing through only if the junub person's own door opens onto the mosque. [1 notes: ABUHAYYAN-FULL(الليث)]
- 4:43/q13/p15 if الصلاة means its locations, the phrase permits people in janāba to pass through mosques, because there is no other passage, or for another reason according to the disagreement — SAMIN-DURR, d. 756 (author): If الصلاة means its locations, the phrase permits people in janāba to pass through mosques, because there is no other passage, or for another reason according to the disagreement. [1 notes: SAMIN-DURR]
- 4:43/q13/p16 the verse forbids prayer while intoxicated enough not to know one's words and forbids a junub person from entering a mosque except to pass through without staying; this preceded the final wine prohibition — IBNKATHIR-FULL, d. 774 (author): The verse forbids prayer while intoxicated enough not to know one's words and forbids a junub person from entering a mosque except to pass through without staying; this preceded the final wine prohibition. [1 notes: IBNKATHIR-FULL]
- 4:43/q13/p17 yazid ibn abi habib says the verse addressed anṣar whose homes opened into the mosque: when junub and without water, they had to cross the mosque to reach water — IBNKATHIR-FULL, d. 774 (يزيد بن أبي حبيب): Yazid ibn Abi Habib says the verse addressed Anṣar whose homes opened into the mosque: when junub and without water, they had to cross the mosque to reach water. [1 notes: IBNKATHIR-FULL(يزيد بن أبي حبيب)]
- 4:43/q13/p18 ibn kathir rejects reports that the prophet ordered mosque doors closed except ali's as mistaken; the sound report names abu bakr — IBNKATHIR-FULL, d. 774 (author): Ibn Kathir rejects reports that the Prophet ordered mosque doors closed except Ali's as mistaken; the sound report names Abu Bakr. [1 notes: IBNKATHIR-FULL]
- 4:43/q13/p19 many imams say a junub person may not remain in a mosque but may pass through; menstruating and postpartum women are analogous — IBNKATHIR-FULL, d. 774 (author): Many imams say a junub person may not remain in a mosque but may pass through; menstruating and postpartum women are analogous. [1 notes: IBNKATHIR-FULL]
- 4:43/q13/p20 some authorities prohibit menstruating and postpartum women from passing through a mosque because of possible contamination — IBNKATHIR-FULL, d. 774 (بعض الأئمة): Some authorities prohibit menstruating and postpartum women from passing through a mosque because of possible contamination. [1 notes: IBNKATHIR-FULL(بعض الأئمة)]
- 4:43/q13/p21 aisha reports that when she said she was menstruating after the prophet asked her to pass him a prayer mat from the mosque, he said menstruation was not in her hand; abu hurayra reports a — IBNKATHIR-FULL, d. 774 (عائشة): Aisha reports that when she said she was menstruating after the Prophet asked her to pass him a prayer mat from the mosque, he said menstruation was not in her hand; Abu Hurayra reports a similar exchange. Muslim transmits both, supporting a menstruating woman's passage. [1 notes: IBNKATHIR-FULL(عائشة)]
- 4:43/q13/p22 a report transmitted by abu dawud through aflat ibn khalifa and jasra from aisha says a menstruating or junub person is not permitted in the mosque; ibn majah has a parallel route through umm salama — IBNKATHIR-FULL, d. 774 (رسول الله صلى الله عليه وسلم): A report transmitted by Abu Dawud through Aflat ibn Khalifa and Jasra from Aisha says a menstruating or junub person is not permitted in the mosque; Ibn Majah has a parallel route through Umm Salama. [1 notes: IBNKATHIR-FULL(رسول الله صلى الله عليه وسلم)]
- 4:43/q13/p23 al-tirmidhi transmits a report telling ali that only the prophet and ali may be junub in the mosque; ibn kathir says it is weak and unestablished because salim ibn abi hafsa is abandoned and atiyya — IBNKATHIR-FULL, d. 774 (رسول الله صلى الله عليه وسلم): Al-Tirmidhi transmits a report telling Ali that only the Prophet and Ali may be junub in the mosque; Ibn Kathir says it is weak and unestablished because Salim ibn Abi Hafsa is abandoned and Atiyya is weak. [1 notes: IBNKATHIR-FULL(رسول الله صلى الله عليه وسلم)]
- 4:43/q13/p24 a junub may pass through the mosque without stopping or praying — BIQAI-FULL, d. 885 (author): A junub may pass through the mosque without stopping or praying. [1 notes: BIQAI-FULL]
- 4:43/q13/p25 jabir reports that people among them would cross the mosque while junub, passing through rather than staying — DURR-FULL, d. 911 (جابر): Jabir reports that people among them would cross the mosque while junub, passing through rather than staying. [1 notes: DURR-FULL(جابر)]
- 4:43/q13/p26 ibn abbas says the junub may cross a mosque but must not remain seated there — DURR-FULL, d. 911 (ابن عباس): Ibn Abbas says the junub may cross a mosque but must not remain seated there. [1 notes: DURR-FULL(ابن عباس)]
- 4:43/q13/p27 yazid ibn abi habib says the verse was revealed because some anṣār had mosque doors but no route to water except through the mosque when they became junub — DURR-FULL, d. 911 (يزيد بن أبي حبيب): Yazid ibn Abi Habib says the verse was revealed because some Anṣār had mosque doors but no route to water except through the mosque when they became junub. [1 notes: DURR-FULL(يزيد بن أبي حبيب)]
- 4:43/q13/p28 ibn abbas allows menstruants and junub people to pass through the mosque provided they do not sit there — DURR-FULL, d. 911 (ابن عباس): Ibn Abbas allows menstruants and junub people to pass through the mosque provided they do not sit there. [1 notes: DURR-FULL(ابن عباس)]
- 4:43/q13/p29 abu ubayda says the junub may pass through the mosque but may not sit in it — DURR-FULL, d. 911 (أبو عبيدة): Abu Ubayda says the junub may pass through the mosque but may not sit in it. [1 notes: DURR-FULL(أبو عبيدة)]
- 4:43/q13/p30 ata says the junub may pass through the mosque — DURR-FULL, d. 911 (عطاء): Ata says the junub may pass through the mosque. [1 notes: DURR-FULL(عطاء)]
- 4:43/q13/p31 anas says a junub person may pass through the mosque but not remain there — DURR-FULL, d. 911 (أنس): Anas says a junub person may pass through the mosque but not remain there. [1 notes: DURR-FULL(أنس)]
- 4:43/q13/p32 yazīd b. abī ḥabīb says some anṣār had doors opening into the mosque and, lacking water while impure, had to cross the mosque to reach it; the passing-through clause was revealed — SUYUTI-LUBAB, d. 911 (يزيد بن أبي حبيب): Yazīd b. Abī Ḥabīb says some Anṣār had doors opening into the mosque and, lacking water while impure, had to cross the mosque to reach it; the passing-through clause was revealed. [1 notes: SUYUTI-LUBAB(يزيد بن أبي حبيب)]
- 4:43/q13/p33 al-layth allows a junub person to pass only when his door opens into the mosque; some ansar had no other route, so they received a concession — ALUSI-FULL, d. 1270 (الليث): Al-Layth allows a junub person to pass only when his door opens into the mosque; some Ansar had no other route, so they received a concession. [1 notes: ALUSI-FULL(الليث)]
- 4:43/q13/p34 a report is cited for ali allowing passage through a mosque for the junub; al-alusi says the transmission that ali first allowed it and later prohibited it is not established to him — ALUSI-FULL, d. 1270 (علي): A report is cited for Ali allowing passage through a mosque for the junub; al-Alusi says the transmission that Ali first allowed it and later prohibited it is not established to him. [1 notes: ALUSI-FULL(علي)]
- 4:43/q13/p35 by indication, the verse also bars drunk and junub people from entering or even approaching the mosque; the exception for passing through applies only to junub people, not the drunk — ELMALILI, d. 1361 (author): By indication, the verse also bars drunk and junub people from entering or even approaching the mosque; the exception for passing through applies only to junub people, not the drunk. [1 notes: ELMALILI]
- 4:43/q13/p36 the phrase may also permit a junub person to pass along the road beside a mosque, but not enter the mosque; it has no bearing on drunk people — ELMALILI, d. 1361 (author): The phrase may also permit a junub person to pass along the road beside a mosque, but not enter the mosque; it has no bearing on drunk people. [1 notes: ELMALILI]
- 4:43/q13/p37 some scholars understand “passing through” as passing through a mosque, on the premise that “prayer” means mosque; the exception then permits a junub person to cross without staying — IBNASHUR-FULL, d. 1393 (بعض العلماء): Some scholars understand “passing through” as passing through a mosque, on the premise that “prayer” means mosque; the exception then permits a junub person to cross without staying. [1 notes: IBNASHUR-FULL(بعض العلماء)]
- 4:43/q13/p38 a claim that ʿalī’s daughter’s wicket remained in the mosque is also reported, but ibn ʿashur says it is unsound — IBNASHUR-FULL, d. 1393 (author): A claim that ʿAlī’s daughter’s wicket remained in the mosque is also reported, but Ibn ʿAshur says it is unsound. [1 notes: IBNASHUR-FULL]
- 4:43/q13/p39 ibn ashur says umar and ibn masud understood passing through as permission to pass through a mosque and took touching women as literal, wudu-breaking contact, as al-shafiʿi does — IBNASHUR-FULL, d. 1393 (author): Ibn Ashur says Umar and Ibn Masud understood passing through as permission to pass through a mosque and took touching women as literal, wudu-breaking contact, as al-Shafiʿi does. [1 notes: IBNASHUR-FULL]
- 4:43/q13/p40 because prayers were commonly performed congregationally in mosques and the verse also addresses a junub's mosque entry, its wording is compressed in this way — TABATABAI, d. 1402 (author): Because prayers were commonly performed congregationally in mosques and the verse also addresses a junub's mosque entry, its wording is compressed in this way. [1 notes: TABATABAI]

Reports say the Prophet redirected the routes, with exceptions. [1 notes: KURANYOLU-TEFSIR]
- 4:43/q13/p42 those scholars allow junub people to enter and exit a mosque for a need if they do not sit there — KURANYOLU-TEFSIR (İbn Mes‘ûd, İbn Dînâr, Nehaî, Mâlik ve Şâfiî): Those scholars allow junub people to enter and exit a mosque for a need if they do not sit there. [1 notes: KURANYOLU-TEFSIR(İbn Mes‘ûd, İbn Dînâr, Nehaî, Mâlik ve Şâfiî)]

## 4:43/q14 What illness permits tayammum?  [مَّرْضَىٰٓ · law]
turns on: The cited interpretations, reports, language arguments, and legal evidence differ.
- 4:43/q14/p1 Illness named as a qualifying state — TABRISI, d. 548 (author): Coming from the ghait is a euphemism for relieving oneself; or can mean and, because illness and travel permit tayammum while using the toilet requires purification. | mentions 37:147 QURTUBI-FULL, d. 671 (ابن عباس): A second report from Ibn ʿAbbas says the sick person was granted permission to use clean earth for tayammum. QURTUBI-FULL, d. 671 (داود): Dawud reads the term sick literally: anyone who can be called ill may use tayammum. QURTUBI-FULL, d. 671 (أبو يوسف وزفر): Abu Yusuf and Zufar prohibit tayammum for residents, whether the reason is illness or fear that prayer time will end. QURTUBI-FULL, d. 671 (مالك): Malik treats illness and travel as the usual cases where water is absent: residents and healthy people may analogically use tayammum if water is unavailable, obstructed, or time would pass. QURTUBI-FULL, d. 671 (author): Al-Qurtubi prefers aw in its ordinary sense, with ellipsis: illness preventing water use, or travel with no water and a need for it. BAYDAWI-FULL, d. 685 (author): The illness may make water use dangerous or prevent reaching it; in either case, the person is treated as lacking water. NASAFI-FULL, d. 710 (author): The dry-ablution command covers the sick, travelers, people with minor impurity, and those in major impurity; it applies when each lacks usable water for the relevant reason. ABUHAYYAN-FULL, d. 745 (author): Not finding water normally means searching without reaching it; for the sick, existing water that cannot be used counts legally as absent. ABUHAYYAN-FULL, d. 745 (الشافعي): Al-Shāfiʿī's stated position disallows tayammum for that fear, though his correct view is reported to permit it when long illness is feared. ABUHAYYAN-FULL, d. 745 (الزمخشري): Al-Zamakhsharī explains that illness and travel are named first because they commonly create need for the concession, then the rule extends to anyone obliged to purify but lacking water through an enemy, beast, missing drawing tool, isolation, or similar cause. IBNKATHIR-FULL, d. 774 (بعض العلماء): Some scholars permit tayammum for illness generally, relying on the verse’s broad wording. IBNKATHIR-FULL, d. 774 (author): The verse guards prayer from impairment by drunkenness, junub status, or minor impurity, while making a merciful tayammum exception for illness or lack of water. DURR-FULL, d. 911 (مجاهد): Mujahid says a sick person who becomes junub and fears for his life receives the same tayammum concession as a traveler without water. SUYUTI-LUBAB, d. 911 (مجاهد): Mujāhid says the clause about illness was revealed for an Anṣārī who could not rise to wash and had no servant to bring water, so he told the Prophet. BURSEVI, d. 1127 (author): Jurists permit dry ablution in the first two illness categories but not the third, where only a blemish or defect is feared. ALUSI-FULL, d. 1270 (author): The wording's general expression followed by detail is more eloquent and emphatic than explicitly listing travelers and patients lacking water, while accommodating different levels of understanding. ALUSI-FULL, d. 1270 (author): Illness means any condition that prevents water use, whether because water cannot be reached or cannot safely be used. ALUSI-FULL, d. 1270 (قيل): Travel is mentioned to connect illness with travel and equate them through inability to use water, even when water is present. ALUSI-FULL, d. 1270 (بعضهم): A proposal reorders the verse: prohibit prayer in intoxication, janaba, or after either event, then command tayammum for illness or travel; al-Alusi objects that it splits condition and result without rationale. ALUSI-FULL, d. 1270 (author): Al-Alusi prefers the apparent order: waterlessness can mean inability to use water and govern all cases, or retain its literal sense without attaching it to illness and travel. ALUSI-FULL, d. 1270 (author): Illness and travel need not be restricted to people already in minor or major impurity: the verse explains the switch from water purification to tayammum. ALUSI-FULL, d. 1270 (author): People outside illness and travel illustrate that inability to use water for other reasons also licenses tayammum; the ritual event itself is not the cause. ALUSI-FULL, d. 1270 (قيل): One explanation says water absence also conditions illness and travel, but need not be repeated because major impurity is already clear there and minor impurity follows by textual implication. IBNASHUR-FULL, d. 1393 (مالك): In the Baghdadi transmission, a patient unable to use water may perform multiple prayers with one tayammum until a nullifier; this also applies to missed prayers, and no Malik report addresses a junub person who then loses wudu. IBNASHUR-FULL, d. 1393 (author): The water condition does not restrict the sick person's tayammum, because the meaning of illness already indicates the concession. IBNASHUR-FULL, d. 1393 (author): The sick person is not merely someone chronically bedridden, since someone can usually bring water to such a person. ASAD-NOTES, d. 1412 (author): When water is unavailable or cannot be used due to illness, tayammum replaces both the full bath after intercourse and the partial pre-prayer ablution. KURANYOLU-TEFSIR (author): Illness or travel alone does not grant the tayammum dispensation; water must also be unavailable or unusable. KURANYOLU-TEFSIR (author): Because residents and healthy people may also perform tayammum when water is absent, the commentary asks why illness and travel are singled out. KURANYOLU-TEFSIR (author): Another answer is that illness and travel are singled out because water is more likely to be absent or unusable then. STUDYQURAN (author): Clean earth substitutes for water when illness prevents seeking or safely using water, or on a journey where water is unavailable. | mentions 5:6 STUDYQURAN (legal schools): Schools differ on water-search requirements, illness threshold, whether tayammum removes impurity or temporarily permits prayer, and whether prayer must be repeated if water is found before prayer time ends. MUQATIL (source note MUQATIL:4:43/r6) supports this position. TAB-FULL (source note TAB-FULL:v7p61#2/r1) supports this position. TAB-FULL (source note TAB-FULL:v7p61#2/r2) supports this position. TAB-FULL (source note TAB-FULL:v7p61#2/r3) supports this position. TAB-FULL (source note TAB-FULL:v7p61/r1) supports this position. TAB-FULL (source note TAB-FULL:v7p61/r2) supports this position. THALABI (source note THALABI:v3p318/r8) supports this position. THALABI (source note THALABI:v3p319/r1) supports this position. THALABI (source note THALABI:v3p319/r2) supports this position. MAWARDI-FULL (source note MAWARDI-FULL:v1p489/r9) supports this position. MAWARDI-FULL (source note MAWARDI-FULL:v1p489/r10) supports this position. BAGHAWI-FULL (source note BAGHAWI-FULL:v2p221/r3) supports this position. BAGHAWI-FULL (source note BAGHAWI-FULL:v2p221/r4) supports this position. BAGHAWI-FULL (source note BAGHAWI-FULL:v2p221/r5) supports this position. BAGHAWI-FULL (source note BAGHAWI-FULL:v2p222/r1) supports this position. BAGHAWI-FULL (source note BAGHAWI-FULL:v2p222/r2) supports this position. IBNATIYYA-FULL (source note IBNATIYYA-FULL:v2p58/r1) supports this position. IBNATIYYA-FULL (source note IBNATIYYA-FULL:v2p58/r2) supports this position. IBNATIYYA-FULL (source note IBNATIYYA-FULL:v2p58/r3) supports this position. IBNATIYYA-FULL (source note IBNATIYYA-FULL:v2p58/r4) supports this position. [74 notes: TABRISI; QURTUBI-FULL(أبو يوسف وزفر,ابن عباس,داود,مالك); BAYDAWI-FULL; NASAFI-FULL; ABUHAYYAN-FULL(الزمخشري,الشافعي); IBNKATHIR-FULL(بعض العلماء); DURR-FULL(مجاهد); SUYUTI-LUBAB(مجاهد); BURSEVI; ALUSI-FULL(بعضهم,قيل); IBNASHUR-FULL(مالك); ASAD-NOTES; KURANYOLU-TEFSIR; STUDYQURAN(legal schools); MUQATIL; TAB-FULL(أبو مالك,إبراهيم,ابن زيد,ابن مسعود,السدي,الشعبي,الضحاك,سعيد بن جبير,مجاهد); ZAJJAJ(النبي); IBNABIHATIM(ابن عباس,عطاء,عطاء الخراساني); NAHHAS; THALABI(أبو حنيفة,جابر,جماعة من الفقهاء,عطاء والحسن); MAWARDI-FULL(الشافعي,داود بن علي,مالك، الشافعي); WAHIDI-WAJIZ; DAMGHANI; JISHUMI(ابن مسعود والضحاك والسدي وإبراهيم ومجاهد وقتادة,الحسن وابن زيد); BAGHAWI-FULL(أصحاب الرأي,النبي صلى الله عليه وسلم); IBNATIYYA-FULL(داود); ALUSI-FULL+; QURTUBI-FULL+]
- 4:43/q14/p2 Water-use harm or delayed recovery qualifies — TABRISI, d. 548 (ابن عباس وابن مسعود والسدي والضحاك ومجاهد وقتادة): One view permits tayammum for wounds, fractures, and sores when the person fears harm from water. QURTUBI-FULL, d. 671 (author): Within the Maliki school, even less severe illness permits tayammum when water risks causing, worsening, or prolonging illness; Ibn ʿAtiyya says this is what he recalls as consensus. QURTUBI-FULL, d. 671 (أبو حنيفة): Al-Baji reports that Qadi Abu al-Hasan and Abu Hanifa permit tayammum for a healthy person fearing a cold or fever, and for a patient fearing worsening illness. QURTUBI-FULL, d. 671 (ابن العربي): Ibn al-ʿArabi argues that worsening illness is uncertain, so it cannot justify abandoning the certain duty to use water. QURTUBI-FULL, d. 671 (ابن عطية): Ibn ʿAtiyya calls Dawud's view an outlier; scholars mean illness harmed by water, such as smallpox or fever, as in Ibn ʿAbbas's report. ABUHAYYAN-FULL, d. 745 (العلماء): Other jurists restrict illness to conditions such as smallpox, measles, or dangerous ailments where water may harm; they agree tayammum is allowed when harm is feared. ABUHAYYAN-FULL, d. 745 (أبو حنيفة ومالك): Abū Ḥanīfa and Mālik permit tayammum when one fears illness will begin or worsen, or recovery will be delayed. IBNKATHIR-FULL, d. 774 (author): Illness permits tayammum when using water risks losing or damaging a limb, or prolonging recovery. DURR-FULL, d. 911 (ابن مسعود): Ibn Masud says the sick allowed tayammum include the broken and wounded; when junub, they should not wash a wound if doing so risks harm. ALUSI-FULL, d. 1270 (ابن مسعود): Ibn Masud says the patient allowed dry ablution includes a person with a fracture or wound; if he becomes junub, he should not wash an injury when that risks harm. ALUSI-FULL, d. 1270 (ابن عباس): Ibn Abbas, in a report raised to the Prophet, says a person with a wound, ulcers, or smallpox who fears washing while junub may use dry ablution. ALUSI-FULL, d. 1270 (author): The verse's unrestricted wording appears to allow every sick person dry ablution, but other verses exclude those whose illness will not worsen; legal works detail the rule. KURANYOLU-TEFSIR (author): Not finding water can figuratively mean that water exists but cannot be used because of need, fear, or harm; such circumstances are more common for travelers and the sick. [13 notes: TABRISI(ابن عباس وابن مسعود والسدي والضحاك ومجاهد وقتادة); QURTUBI-FULL(أبو حنيفة,ابن العربي,ابن عطية); ABUHAYYAN-FULL(أبو حنيفة ومالك,العلماء); IBNKATHIR-FULL; DURR-FULL(ابن مسعود); ALUSI-FULL(ابن عباس,ابن مسعود); KURANYOLU-TEFSIR]
- 4:43/q14/p3 Fear of death or loss of limb — IBNJAWZI-ZAD, d. 597 (القاضي أبو يعلى): Qāḍī Abū Yaʿlā says illness allows tayammum when using water would cause harm, whether or not death is feared; travel allows it with no water on short or long journeys. Lack of water is not required for the ill, but harm is; it is required for travelers, while travel itself is not a condition because water is commonly absent on journeys. QURTUBI-FULL, d. 671 (الحسن وعطاء): Severe illness that risks death from water or loss of a limb permits tayammum by consensus; al-Hasan and ʿAtaʾ dissent, but al-Qurtubi rejects their view by the no-hardship and no-self-killing verses. | mentions 22:78, 4:29 QURTUBI-FULL, d. 671 (author): Al-Qurtubi answers that if fear of death from cold permits tayammum, fear of illness should also suffice, since both illness and death are harms. QURTUBI-FULL, d. 671 (أبو نصر عبد الرحيم القشيري): Al-Qushayri gives the sound Shafiʿi position: fear of death or losing a limb permits tayammum, and the sound view also permits it to avoid prolonged illness. QURTUBI-FULL, d. 671 (author): Since the Prophet allowed travelling ʿAmr to use tayammum when bathing risked death, a sick person is even more entitled to it. DURR-FULL, d. 911 (ابن عباس): A report raised to the Prophet applies the tayammum concession to a person with war wounds, sores, or smallpox who fears bathing would kill him. BURSEVI, d. 1127 (author): The verse gives three illness levels: water would cause death; it would cause severe pain, worsen or prolong illness; or it would leave only a feared blemish or bodily defect. ALUSI-FULL, d. 1270 (author): The established Hanafi rule permits dry ablution when using water is expected to worsen illness, whether worsening comes from movement or water itself; fear of death is not required. IBNASHUR-FULL, d. 1393 (جمهور العلماء): All other jurists reject Umar and Ibn Masud's restriction; the majority allow tayammum for lack of water or fear that washing will cause death, illness, or worsening, even a cold or fever. IBNASHUR-FULL, d. 1393 (الشافعي): Al-Shafiʿi permits tayammum only for lack of water or fear of death, reasoning that worsening illness is uncertain; Ibn Ashur replies that both outcomes are uncertain and God imposes no hardship. [10 notes: IBNJAWZI-ZAD(القاضي أبو يعلى); QURTUBI-FULL(الحسن وعطاء); DURR-FULL(ابن عباس); BURSEVI; ALUSI-FULL; IBNASHUR-FULL(الشافعي,جمهور العلماء); QURTUBI-FULL(أبو نصر عبد الرحيم القشيري)+]
- 4:43/q14/p4 Inability to obtain or handle water — TABRISI, d. 548 (author): The condition covers sick people and travelers: a traveler lacks water, while a sick person may have no one to wash him or fear harm from water. TABRISI, d. 548 (الحسن وابن زيد): Another view permits it when illness makes someone unable to handle water and no one can bring it; Hasan did not allow an injured person to use tayammum. QURTUBI-FULL, d. 671 (author): Illness counts when no one can hand over water or its use is feared harmful; a healthy resident may also lack usable water through widespread exorbitant prices, imprisonment, or restraint. DURR-FULL, d. 911 (ابن زيد): Ibn Zayd says a sick person without anyone to bring water, who cannot reach it and has no helper, may perform tayammum and pray. [4 notes: TABRISI(الحسن وابن زيد); QURTUBI-FULL; DURR-FULL(ابن زيد)]
- 4:43/q14/p5 Any illness qualifies — ABUHAYYAN-FULL, d. 745 (داود): Dāwūd applies the general wording of illness without limits on severity or duration and allows tayammum to anyone who fits the name sick. [1 notes: ABUHAYYAN-FULL(داود)]

## 4:43/q15 What journeys qualify for tayammum?  [عَلَىٰ سَفَرٍ · law]
turns on: The cited interpretations, reports, language arguments, and legal evidence differ.
- 4:43/q15/p1 Travel when water is unavailable — IBNATIYYA-FULL, d. 542 (فرقة): Another group disallows tayammum during sinful travel; Ibn ʿAṭiyya also judges this weak. IBNATIYYA-FULL, d. 542 (author): A traveler may lack water because none exists, searching risks missing companions, baggage is endangered, or predators or other harm threaten. TABRISI, d. 548 (علي وابن عباس وسعيد بن جبير ومجاهد): One interpretation forbids prayer in janaba except for travelers, who may pray using tayammum even though it does not remove janaba. RAZI-FULL, d. 606 (أصحاب الشافعي): They argue the travel and water ruling follows as a separate rule: readers prefer pausing at ‘until you wash,’ so the earlier exception should not depend on the later verse’s condition. RAZI-FULL, d. 606 (author): A traveler who lacks water must use tayammum whether the journey is long or short. QURTUBI-FULL, d. 671 (author): Scholars agree tayammum is allowed while travelling, but disagree about residence: Malik, his followers, Abu Hanifa, and Muhammad allow it there too. QURTUBI-FULL, d. 671 (ابن عمر): Ibn Umar reportedly would not leave his travel route to seek water even when it lay two bowshots away; Ishaq says one need search only where one is. QURTUBI-FULL, d. 671 (author): A traveler may tayammum if water is entirely or partly unavailable, or seeking it risks losing companions or baggage, encountering thieves or beasts, missing prayer time, or causing thirst to oneself or another; cooking necessary food also counts. QURTUBI-FULL, d. 671 (أبو سعيد الخدري): In the report of Abu Said, two travelers pray after tayammum; the Prophet says the one who does not repeat followed the Sunna and the one who repeats earns double reward. QURTUBI-FULL, d. 671 (أبو داود): Abu Dawud transmits the two-traveler report and notes that the Abu Said link is not preserved in other narrations; al-Daraqutni's version specifies finding water within the prayer time. QURTUBI-FULL, d. 671 (قتادة): Asked about travel tayammum, Qatada reports Ibn Umar’s elbow limit; al-Hasan and Ibrahim al-Nakha‘i also held it. BAYDAWI-FULL, d. 685 (author): The traveler is excused when water is unavailable. BAYDAWI-FULL, d. 685 (author): The exception attaches to “while junub,” exempting travel when water is unavailable and tayammum is used, as the following clause indicates. ABUHAYYAN-FULL, d. 745 (أبو حنيفة): Abū Ḥanīfa allows tayammum for someone who leaves town without intending travel if water is a mile away; another view sets the distance where people's voices cannot be heard. IBNKATHIR-FULL, d. 774 (author): Travel permits tayammum whether the journey is long or short. DURR-FULL, d. 911 (عمار بن ياسر): Ammar ibn Yasir reports that after he became junub while traveling and rolled in dust, the Prophet taught him that tayammum requires only striking the earth and wiping the face and palms. SUYUTI-LUBAB, d. 911 (علي): ʿAlī says the clause about being ritually impure except when passing through concerns a traveler who became impure, performed dry ablution, and prayed. BURSEVI, d. 1127 (author): A traveler excused by travel may pray using dry ablution while junub; the prohibition lasts until washing. ALUSI-FULL, d. 1270 (الهداية): Al-Hidaya allows tayammum for someone without water who is traveling or at least a mile outside town. ELMALILI, d. 1361 (author): Abirin sabil means people traveling on the road or in a state of travel; the exception concerns tayammum. IBNASHUR-FULL, d. 1393 (author): The failure to find water is attached to the conditional clause and limits the traveler, one coming from the privy, and one touching women. KURANYOLU-TEFSIR (author): Although the verse later explicitly refers to travelers, this interpretation says the phrase is repeated because water is less available or more needed during travel. KURANYOLU-TEFSIR (author): Scholars agree tayammum is permissible during travel when water is absent or an obstacle prevents its use. KURANYOLU-TEFSIR (author): The commentary reads this verse as stating the tayammum substitute during travel and 5:6 as stating it for residents. | mentions 5:6 STUDYQURAN (the Prophet’s wife ʿĀʾishah): The verse reportedly followed ʿĀʾishah losing a necklace while traveling with the Prophet. The group camped to search, lacked water, complained they could not wash, and the verse came at dawn prayer. MAJAZ (source note MAJAZ:v1p128/r1) supports this position. THALABI (source note THALABI:v3p319#2/r1) supports this position. THALABI (source note THALABI:v3p319#2/r2) supports this position. MAWARDI-FULL (source note MAWARDI-FULL:v1p489/r12) supports this position. MAWARDI-FULL (source note MAWARDI-FULL:v1p489/r13) supports this position. MAWARDI-FULL (source note MAWARDI-FULL:v1p489/r14) supports this position. WAHIDI-WAJIZ (source note WAHIDI-WAJIZ:4:43-44/r7) supports this position. JISHUMI (source note JISHUMI:v2p1567/r12) supports this position. BAGHAWI-FULL (source note BAGHAWI-FULL:v2p222/r3) supports this position. IBNATIYYA-FULL (source note IBNATIYYA-FULL:v2p58/r5) supports this position. [35 notes: IBNATIYYA-FULL(فرقة,مالك وجمهور الفقهاء); TABRISI(علي وابن عباس وسعيد بن جبير ومجاهد); RAZI-FULL; QURTUBI-FULL(أبو داود,أبو سعيد الخدري,ابن عمر,قتادة); BAYDAWI-FULL; ABUHAYYAN-FULL(أبو حنيفة); IBNKATHIR-FULL; DURR-FULL(عمار بن ياسر); SUYUTI-LUBAB(علي); BURSEVI; ALUSI-FULL(الهداية); ELMALILI; IBNASHUR-FULL; KURANYOLU-TEFSIR; STUDYQURAN(the Prophet’s wife ʿĀʾishah); MAJAZ; THALABI(ابن عمر); MAWARDI-FULL(أبو حنيفة,داود,مالك، الشافعي); WAHIDI-WAJIZ; JISHUMI; BAGHAWI-FULL; RAZI-FULL(أصحاب الشافعي)+]
- 4:43/q15/p2 Only a qualifying journey — IBNATIYYA-FULL, d. 542 (الشافعي وقوم): Al-Shāfiʿī reports a view that tayammum is allowed only on travel that permits shortening prayer; Ibn ʿAṭiyya calls this weak. QURTUBI-FULL, d. 671 (author): When water is unavailable, tayammum is allowed on a journey of any length under Malik and most scholars; requiring a prayer-shortening or devotional journey is weak. [2 notes: IBNATIYYA-FULL(الشافعي وقوم); QURTUBI-FULL]
- 4:43/q15/p3 Any journey, short or long — BURSEVI, d. 1127 (أبو حنيفة): Travel, short or long, is cited to establish the ruling and its procedure; the rule also applies wherever water cannot be used, and Abu Hanifa permits tayammum for janabah in town without hot water. [1 notes: BURSEVI(أبو حنيفة)]

## 4:43/q16 What does al-ghāʾiṭ mean?  [ٱلْغَآئِطِ · meaning]
turns on: The cited interpretations, reports, language arguments, and legal evidence differ.
- 4:43/q16/p1 ghait means a low place where people relieved themselves out of sight; its name became a euphemism for the act or result. another account says it was named for what people discarded there; murajj defines — TABRISI, d. 548 (author): Ghait means a low place where people relieved themselves out of sight; its name became a euphemism for the act or result. Another account says it was named for what people discarded there; Murajj defines it as a basin ringed by hills. MAJAZ (source note MAJAZ:v1p128/r2) supports this position. IBNQUTAYBA-GHARIB (source note IBNQUTAYBA-GHARIB:v1p127#3/r2) supports this position. TAB-FULL (source note TAB-FULL:v7p61#2/r4) supports this position. IBNABIHATIM (source note IBNABIHATIM:v3p960/r5) supports this position. THALABI (source note THALABI:v3p313/r13) supports this position. THALABI (source note THALABI:v3p313/r14) supports this position. MAWARDI-FULL (source note MAWARDI-FULL:v1p489/r15) supports this position. WAHIDI-WAJIZ (source note WAHIDI-WAJIZ:4:43-44/r8) supports this position. JISHUMI (source note JISHUMI:v2p1565/r9) supports this position. JISHUMI (source note JISHUMI:v2p1565/r10) supports this position. JISHUMI (source note JISHUMI:v2p1567/r13) supports this position. BAGHAWI-FULL (source note BAGHAWI-FULL:v2p222#2/r1) supports this position. - 4:43/q16/p2 al-ghāʾiṭ literally means low, level ground; the place is used euphemistically for the act of excretion, like other container or vehicle terms used for their contents — IBNJAWZI-ZAD, d. 597 (ابن قتيبة): Al-Ghāʾiṭ literally means low, level ground; the place is used euphemistically for the act of excretion, like other container or vehicle terms used for their contents. [1 notes: IBNJAWZI-ZAD(ابن قتيبة)]
- 4:43/q16/p3 the author gives parallels: rāwiya is properly the camel carrying water, and ẓaʿāʾin are properly the howdahs carrying women; ʿadhira likewise names excretion from the courtyards where it was left — IBNJAWZI-ZAD, d. 597 (author): The author gives parallels: rāwiya is properly the camel carrying water, and ẓaʿāʾin are properly the howdahs carrying women; ʿadhira likewise names excretion from the courtyards where it was left. [1 notes: IBNJAWZI-ZAD]
- 4:43/q16/p4 al-ghāʾiṭ originally means a low, concealing place; people sought one to relieve themselves out of sight, and the bodily event later took the place’s name. its plural is ghīṭān — RAZI-FULL, d. 606 (author): Al-ghāʾiṭ originally means a low, concealing place; people sought one to relieve themselves out of sight, and the bodily event later took the place’s name. Its plural is ghīṭān. [1 notes: RAZI-FULL]
- 4:43/q16/p5 al-qushayri reads the toilet clause as covering a resident who lacks water, and says the rare residential case raises disagreement about repeating the prayer — QURTUBI-FULL, d. 671 (أبو نصر عبد الرحيم القشيري): Al-Qushayri reads the toilet clause as covering a resident who lacks water, and says the rare residential case raises disagreement about repeating the prayer. [1 notes: QURTUBI-FULL(أبو نصر عبد الرحيم القشيري)]
- 4:43/q16/p6 al-ghaʾit originally meant a hollow in the land; because people used secluded hollows to relieve themselves, the word came to denote human excretion — QURTUBI-FULL, d. 671 (author): Al-ghaʾit originally meant a hollow in the land; because people used secluded hollows to relieve themselves, the word came to denote human excretion. [1 notes: QURTUBI-FULL]
- 4:43/q16/p7 the objection that the verse pairs the toilet event with janaba does not exclude touch: al-qurtubi says the word can carry both touch and intercourse and convey both rulings — QURTUBI-FULL, d. 671 (author): The objection that the verse pairs the toilet event with janaba does not exclude touch: al-Qurtubi says the word can carry both touch and intercourse and convey both rulings. [1 notes: QURTUBI-FULL]
- 4:43/q16/p8 coming from the privy means becoming impure through discharge from either outlet; ghāʾiṭ originally means a low-lying place — BAYDAWI-FULL, d. 685 (author): Coming from the privy means becoming impure through discharge from either outlet; ghāʾiṭ originally means a low-lying place. [1 notes: BAYDAWI-FULL]
- 4:43/q16/p9 al-ghāʾiṭ literally means a low, sunken area where people went to relieve themselves, so it euphemistically denotes defecation — NASAFI-FULL, d. 710 (author): Al-ghāʾiṭ literally means a low, sunken area where people went to relieve themselves, so it euphemistically denotes defecation. [1 notes: NASAFI-FULL]
- 4:43/q16/p10 the low ground where a man sought privacy for defecation lent its name to the act itself, as in metonymic expressions like a river running — ABUHAYYAN-FULL, d. 745 (author): The low ground where a man sought privacy for defecation lent its name to the act itself, as in metonymic expressions like a river running. [1 notes: ABUHAYYAN-FULL]
- 4:43/q16/p11 the shift to third person in جاء أحد delicately avoids directly attributing the toilet need to the addressees, while illness, travel, and touching are said directly — ABUHAYYAN-FULL, d. 745 (author): The shift to third person in جاء أحد delicately avoids directly attributing the toilet need to the addressees, while illness, travel, and touching are said directly. [1 notes: ABUHAYYAN-FULL]
- 4:43/q16/p12 coming from the toilet euphemizes the bodily event and extends to wind, urine, semen, and pre-ejaculatory fluid; these six events all invalidate ablution — ABUHAYYAN-FULL, d. 745 (author): Coming from the toilet euphemizes the bodily event and extends to wind, urine, semen, and pre-ejaculatory fluid; these six events all invalidate ablution. [1 notes: ABUHAYYAN-FULL]
- 4:43/q16/p13 those who take touching literally can read the list as escalation from lesser to greater: illness, travel, relieving oneself, and then touching a woman — ABUHAYYAN-FULL, d. 745 (author): Those who take touching literally can read the list as escalation from lesser to greater: illness, travel, relieving oneself, and then touching a woman. [1 notes: ABUHAYYAN-FULL]
- 4:43/q16/p14 the majority form الغائط originally means low-lying ground, then euphemistically denotes the bodily event; غاط means going far out of sight, whereas تغوط means defecating — SAMIN-DURR, d. 756 (author): The majority form الغائط originally means low-lying ground, then euphemistically denotes the bodily event; غاط means going far out of sight, whereas تغوط means defecating. [1 notes: SAMIN-DURR]
- 4:43/q16/p15 the command’s pronoun covers every previously named person: the sick, traveler, one coming from the toilet, and one who touched or was touched — SAMIN-DURR, d. 756 (author): The command’s pronoun covers every previously named person: the sick, traveler, one coming from the toilet, and one who touched or was touched. [1 notes: SAMIN-DURR]
- 4:43/q16/p16 al-gha’it literally means a low-lying place; it euphemistically denotes defecation and minor ritual impurity — IBNKATHIR-FULL, d. 774 (author): Al-gha’it literally means a low-lying place; it euphemistically denotes defecation and minor ritual impurity. [1 notes: IBNKATHIR-FULL]
- 4:43/q16/p17 though al-ghāʾiṭ originally meant a low place where people went out of sight to relieve themselves, its common use became equivalent to explicit mention; legal instruction needed familiar arab wording — BURHAN, d. 794 (author): Though al-ghāʾiṭ originally meant a low place where people went out of sight to relieve themselves, its common use became equivalent to explicit mention; legal instruction needed familiar Arab wording. [1 notes: BURHAN]
- 4:43/q16/p18 al-ghāʾiṭ literally means a low, broad place used for relieving oneself; here it means returning from relieving oneself after a necessary bodily function — BIQAI-FULL, d. 885 (author): Al-ghāʾiṭ literally means a low, broad place used for relieving oneself; here it means returning from relieving oneself after a necessary bodily function. [1 notes: BIQAI-FULL]
- 4:43/q16/p19 the phrase can cover either skin touching skin or intercourse, whether or not ejaculation occurs; this is placed later because it is less avoidable and less frequent than relieving oneself — BIQAI-FULL, d. 885 (author): The phrase can cover either skin touching skin or intercourse, whether or not ejaculation occurs; this is placed later because it is less avoidable and less frequent than relieving oneself. [1 notes: BIQAI-FULL]
- 4:43/q16/p20 al-ghait is a low, sunken place; coming from there is a euphemism for relieving oneself, since people went there to conceal themselves — BURSEVI, d. 1127 (author): Al-ghait is a low, sunken place; coming from there is a euphemism for relieving oneself, since people went there to conceal themselves. [1 notes: BURSEVI]
- 4:43/q16/p21 al-ghaʾiṭ originally means a low place; going there became a euphemism for relieving oneself because people concealed themselves from view — ALUSI-FULL, d. 1270 (author): Al-ghaʾiṭ originally means a low place; going there became a euphemism for relieving oneself because people concealed themselves from view. [1 notes: ALUSI-FULL]
- 4:43/q16/p22 ibn masʿud reads al-ghayṭ with fatḥa on ghayn and a still yāʾ — ALUSI-FULL, d. 1270 (ابن مسعود): Ibn Masʿud reads al-ghayṭ with fatḥa on ghayn and a still yāʾ. [1 notes: ALUSI-FULL(ابن مسعود)]
- 4:43/q16/p23 one derivation treats ghayṭ as the verbal noun of yaghūṭ, with the expected wāw changed to yāʾ and made still — ALUSI-FULL, d. 1270 (author): One derivation treats ghayṭ as the verbal noun of yaghūṭ, with the expected wāw changed to yāʾ and made still. [1 notes: ALUSI-FULL]
- 4:43/q16/p24 the bodily events trigger purification; lack of water or ability to use it is the actual condition licensing tayammum — ALUSI-FULL, d. 1270 (author): The bodily events trigger purification; lack of water or ability to use it is the actual condition licensing tayammum. [1 notes: ALUSI-FULL]
- 4:43/q16/p25 ghait means a low hollow place and euphemistically refers to a latrine; coming from it means relieving oneself and having minor ritual impurity — ELMALILI, d. 1361 (author): Ghait means a low hollow place and euphemistically refers to a latrine; coming from it means relieving oneself and having minor ritual impurity. [1 notes: ELMALILI]
- 4:43/q16/p26 mentioning discharge from the privy adds tayammum as a substitute for wudu, completing the two kinds of tayammum; other causes can be inferred by analogy — IBNASHUR-FULL, d. 1393 (author): Mentioning discharge from the privy adds tayammum as a substitute for wudu, completing the two kinds of tayammum; other causes can be inferred by analogy. [1 notes: IBNASHUR-FULL]
- 4:43/q16/p27 coming from the privy is a euphemism for relieving oneself, chosen because direct mention was considered repellent — IBNASHUR-FULL, d. 1393 (author): Coming from the privy is a euphemism for relieving oneself, chosen because direct mention was considered repellent. [1 notes: IBNASHUR-FULL]
- 4:43/q16/p28 al-ghāʾiṭ originally means a low place hidden from view; arabs went outside the settlement to such places, then the euphemism became commonplace and jurists used it for the resulting state and its acts — IBNASHUR-FULL, d. 1393 (author): Al-ghāʾiṭ originally means a low place hidden from view; Arabs went outside the settlement to such places, then the euphemism became commonplace and jurists used it for the resulting state and its acts. turns on: The cited interpretations, reports, language arguments, and legal evidence differ.

- 4:43/q18/p12 hamza and al-kisai read لمستم here and in 5:6; the others read لامستم — SAMIN-DURR, d. 756 (حمزة والكسائي): Hamza and al-Kisai read لمستم here and in 5:6; the others read لامستم. | mentions 5:6 [1 notes: SAMIN-DURR(حمزة والكسائي)]
- 4:43/q18/p13 the reading أو لمستم النساء supports scholars who understand لمس as touching; the author adduces quranic usage, a prophetic saying, and a poem whose verb ألمست means touch — NASHR, d. 833 (author): The reading أو لمستم النساء supports scholars who understand لمس as touching; the author adduces Quranic usage, a prophetic saying, and a poem whose verb ألمست means touch. | mentions 4:43, 5:6, 6:7 [1 notes: NASHR]
- 4:43/q18/p14 ibrahim al-nakhaʿi read the phrase as “أو لمستم النساء” and understood it as contact short of intercourse — DURR-FULL, d. 911 (إبراهيم النخعي): Ibrahim al-Nakhaʿi read the phrase as “أو لمستم النساء” and understood it as contact short of intercourse. [1 notes: DURR-FULL(إبراهيم النخعي)]
- 4:43/q18/p15 the literal sense is stronger, especially in hamza and al-kisaʾi's reading aw lamastum, since لمس is less established as a euphemism for intercourse than ملامسة — ALUSI-FULL, d. 1270 (author): The literal sense is stronger, especially in Hamza and al-Kisaʾi's reading aw lamastum, since لمس is less established as a euphemism for intercourse than ملامسة. [1 notes: ALUSI-FULL+]

## 4:43/q19 What does touching women mean?  [لَٰمَسْتُمُ ٱلنِّسَآءَ · meaning]
turns on: The cited interpretations, reports, language arguments, and legal evidence differ.
- 4:43/q19/p1 Both bodily contact and intercourse — TABRISI, d. 548 (ابن عباس): When Arabs and non-Arabs disagreed whether it meant intercourse or touching, Ibn Abbas said the non-Arabs were right: it means intercourse, called touch because it leads to intercourse. TABRISI, d. 548 (علي وابن عباس ومجاهد والسدي وقتادة): One interpretation takes touching women to mean intercourse; Abu Hanifa and al-Jubbaʾi adopt it. IBNJAWZI-ZAD, d. 597 (علي، ابن عباس، الحسن، مجاهد، قتادة): One legal interpretation takes touching women to mean sexual intercourse. RAZI-FULL, d. 606 (أنصار قول الجماع): Supporters of the intercourse interpretation cite Qur’anic euphemisms for intercourse at 2:37 and 58:3; Ibn ʿAbbās says God is modest and noble, veiling the meaning with ‘touching.’ | mentions 2:37, 58:3 RAZI-FULL, d. 606 (أنصار قول الجماع): They also argue that ‘coming from the place of need’ already covers minor impurity; if touching women also meant minor impurity, the verse would omit major impurity, so it must mean intercourse. QURTUBI-FULL, d. 671 (author): The three reported meanings are intercourse, physical contact, or a word covering both; most readers treat the two readings as equivalent in sense. QURTUBI-FULL, d. 671 (محمد بن يزيد): Muhammad b. Yazid says lamastum more naturally means kissing or the like, since each party acts; lamistum means intercourse or touching, where the woman does not act. QURTUBI-FULL, d. 671 (ابن العربي): Ibn al-ʿArabi says the verse assigns three clauses to three rulings: janaba, minor impurity, and touch or kissing; treating touch as intercourse would repeat the meaning. QURTUBI-FULL, d. 671 (author): Although the reciprocal form mulamasa is said to require two actors, al-Qurtubi answers that both parties can be described as toucher and touched; Arabic also uses reciprocal verbs for one actor. QURTUBI-FULL, d. 671 (author): Masḥ can mean intercourse, cutting with a sword, a camel's day's travel, a woman without buttocks, or a touch of beauty; here it means dragging the hand across the object. NASAFI-FULL, d. 710 (علي وابن عباس): Ali and Ibn ʿAbbās explain touching women here as sexual intercourse. ABUHAYYAN-FULL, d. 745 (author): The long-alif form can correspond to the simple verb, like جاوزت الشيء وجزته; its form does not distinguish active from passive though the meaning can, and al-Shāfiʿī applies it to both toucher and touched in his stronger view. SAMIN-DURR, d. 756 (غير مسمى): One interpretation makes فاعل equivalent to a simple verb; another says لمس means intercourse while لامس denotes touching short of intercourse. IBNKATHIR-FULL, d. 774 (ابن عمر وعبيدة وأبو عثمان النهدي وأبو عبيدة وعامر الشعبي وثابت بن الحجاج وإبراهيم النخعي وزيد بن أسلم): Ibn Abi Hatim transmits the interpretation that touch means less than intercourse from these authorities as well. IBNKATHIR-FULL, d. 774 (ناصر هذه المقالة): Advocates of the physical-touch reading cite Quran 6:7, prophetic usage toward Ma‘iz, the hadith about the hand’s zina, Aisha’s report of the Prophet kissing and touching his wives, and the Sahihayn prohibition of touch-sale; they say Arabic uses the word for both touch and intercourse. | mentions 6:7 IBNKATHIR-FULL, d. 774 (ابن عباس وعلي وأبي بن كعب ومجاهد وطاوس والحسن وعبيد بن عمير وسعيد بن جبير والشعبي وقتادة ومقاتل بن حيان): One interpretation takes touching women as a euphemism for intercourse, held by these authorities; Quranic uses of touching in divorce contexts support that sense. | mentions 2:237, 33:49 BURHAN, d. 794 (author): The Qur'an euphemizes intercourse with terms for touching, intimacy, marital union, entry, and covering, drawing on bodily contact and clothing or cultivation metaphors. BIQAI-FULL, d. 885 (الشافعي): Al-Shāfiʿī allows lamastum to mean literal skin contact, intercourse caused by contact, or a particular kind of touching; intercourse can be named by its cause or the whole by a part. DURR-FULL, d. 911 (علي بن أبي طالب): Ali says “touching women” means sexual intercourse, expressed euphemistically by God. DURR-FULL, d. 911 (ابن عباس): Ibn Abbas glosses touching women as sexual intercourse. DURR-FULL, d. 911 (ابن عباس): In a discussion, Saʿid ibn Jubayr, Ata, and the non-Arab clients argued for hand-touch, while Ubayd ibn Umayr and the Arabs argued for intercourse; Ibn Abbas judged the Arabs right, since touch-related terms can euphemize intercourse. DURR-FULL, d. 911 (ابن عباس): Ibn Abbas tells Nafi ibn al-Azraq that the verse means intercourse, though Hudhayl uses the word for hand-touch; he cites Labid and al-Aʿsha's poems as evidence for that Arabic usage. ITQAN, d. 911 (author): The reported difference over whether لامستم means intercourse or touching by hand is no disagreement: intercourse fits لامستم, and hand-touch fits لمستم. BURSEVI, d. 1127 (author): Touching women here means having intercourse with them. ALUSI-FULL, d. 1270 (author): “Touching women” euphemistically means intercourse, indicating major impurity, while the preceding clause indicates minor impurity. ALUSI-FULL, d. 1270 (ابن مسعود والنخعي والشعبي): Ibn Masʿud, al-Nakhaʿi, and al-Shaʿbi interpret the phrase as skin touching short of intercourse. ALUSI-FULL, d. 1270 (بعضهم): Others prefer interpreting both readings as intercourse, privileging figurative usage and reconciling the readings; this matches the Hanafi position. IBNASHUR-FULL, d. 1393 (author): Touch literally means bodily contact, and metaphorically can mean seeking or finding, descending, or sexual intercourse; the cited poem witnesses the sense of descending, while 2:237 uses it for intercourse. | mentions 72:8, 2:237 KURANYOLU-TEFSIR (author): Lems and mulamasa literally mean touching with a hand or part of the body, while figuratively they mean sexual intercourse. [71 notes: TABRISI(ابن عباس,علي وابن عباس ومجاهد والسدي وقتادة); IBNJAWZI-ZAD(علي، ابن عباس، الحسن، مجاهد، قتادة); RAZI-FULL(أنصار قول الجماع); QURTUBI-FULL(ابن العربي,محمد بن يزيد); NASAFI-FULL(علي وابن عباس); ABUHAYYAN-FULL; SAMIN-DURR(غير مسمى); IBNKATHIR-FULL(ابن عباس وعلي وأبي بن كعب ومجاهد وطاوس والحسن وعبيد بن عمير وسعيد بن جبير والشعبي وقتادة ومقاتل بن حيان,ابن عمر وعبيدة وأبو عثمان النهدي وأبو عبيدة وعامر الشعبي وثابت بن الحجاج وإبراهيم النخعي وزيد بن أسلم,ناصر هذه المقالة); BURHAN; BIQAI-FULL(الشافعي); DURR-FULL(ابن عباس,علي بن أبي طالب); ITQAN; BURSEVI; ALUSI-FULL(ابن مسعود والنخعي والشعبي,بعضهم); IBNASHUR-FULL; KURANYOLU-TEFSIR; TAB-FULL(آخرون,أبو عبيدة,أم سلمة,إبراهيم,ابن عباس,ابن عمر,الحكم، حماد، عطاء,الشاعر,النبي,سعيد بن جبير,سعيد بن جبير، عطاء، عبيد بن عمير,عائشة,عامر,عبد الله,عبد الله بن مسعود,عبيدة,علي,قتادة والحسن); IBNABIHATIM(عبد الله); THALABI(ابن مسعود وابن عمر وأبو عبيدة ومنصور وعبيدة والشعبي والنخعي وحماد والحكم,عبد الله بن مسعود); MAWARDI-FULL(ابن مسعود، ابن عمر، عبيدة، النخعي، الشعبي، عطاء، ابن سيرين، الشافعي); WAHIDI-WAJIZ; JISHUMI(ابن عباس,ابن عباس والحسن ومجاهد وقتادة وأبو علي,ابن مسعود وابن عمر والشعبي والنخعي وعطاء,عمر وابن مسعود); BAGHAWI-FULL(ابن عباس والحسن ومجاهد وقتادة,ابن مسعود وابن عمر والشعبي والنخعي)]
- 4:43/q19/p2 Sexual intercourse — TABRISI, d. 548 (author): Al-Tabrisi prefers intercourse: the verse already addresses the junub when water is present and must state the junub’s rule when absent as well as the rule for one who relieved himself. RAZI-FULL, d. 606 (ابن مسعود وابن عمر والشعبي والنخعي والشافعي): Ibn Masʿūd, Ibn ʿUmar, al-Shaʿbī, al-Nakhaʿī, and al-Shāfiʿī understand the phrase as skin contact, with or without intercourse. RAZI-FULL, d. 606 (ابن عباس): Ibn ʿAbbās explains that God is modest and noble and uses euphemism, calling sexual intimacy ‘touching.’ QURTUBI-FULL, d. 671 (author): Against the claim that Ibn ʿAbbas interpreted mulamasa as intercourse, al-Qurtubi notes that ʿUmar, his son, and Ibn Masʿud opposed it. QURTUBI-FULL, d. 671 (author): In the cited poetic usage, junub means a stranger; janāba also denotes a man's intercourse with a woman. BAYDAWI-FULL, d. 685 (author): Alternatively, the phrase means sexual intercourse; lamasa is less commonly used as a euphemism for intercourse than mulāmasa. ABUHAYYAN-FULL, d. 745 (علي وابن عباس والحسن ومجاهد وقتادة): ʿAlī, Ibn ʿAbbās, al-Ḥasan, Mujāhid, and Qatāda interpret the phrase as intercourse, in which case a junub may use tayammum. ABUHAYYAN-FULL, d. 745 (الزمخشري): Al-Zamakhsharī takes لمس النساء as intercourse resulting in janāba, following the Ḥanafī school, and reports no other school's view. IBNKATHIR-FULL, d. 774 (ابن عباس): Ibn Kathir says Ibn Abbas’s intercourse interpretation is soundly transmitted through multiple routes. IBNKATHIR-FULL, d. 774 (ابن عباس): Ibn Abbas explains lamastum as intercourse, saying that God may use whatever euphemism He wills; Sa‘id ibn Jubayr reports Ibn Abbas’s response to the disagreement between Arab and non-Arab groups. DURR-FULL, d. 911 (أبو عبيدة): Abu Ubayda says the contact means what is short of intercourse. DURR-FULL, d. 911 (الشعبي): Al-Shabi says the contact means what is short of intercourse. DURR-FULL, d. 911 (الحسن): Al-Hasan interprets the contact as sexual intercourse. ALUSI-FULL, d. 1270 (علي وابن عباس والحسن): ʿAli, Ibn ʿAbbas, and al-Hasan are cited for the intercourse reading, which indicates major impurity. ALUSI-FULL, d. 1270 (بعض المحققين): Some investigators say mutual bodily contact by any body part is the literal meaning, which includes intercourse; it is metaphor only when intercourse alone is intended. IBNASHUR-FULL, d. 1393 (author): Ibn Ashur prefers the euphemistic sense of intercourse: the list gathers common causes of minor and major purification, and the concession context calls for explicit mention rather than relying on implication. ASAD-NOTES, d. 1412 (author): Junub describes the state after sexual intercourse; from janaba, “to make remote,” it denotes distance from prayer due to immersion in sexual passion. KURANYOLU-TEFSIR (author): The synonymous word mess is also used for intercourse in 2:236–237. | mentions 2:236, 2:237 QIRAAT-ER (Ḥanafī): The Ḥanafī view takes Form III mutual engagement as a euphemism for intercourse. MUJAHID (source note MUJAHID:v1p158/r3) supports this position. MUQATIL (source note MUQATIL:4:43/r8) supports this position. MAJAZ (source note MAJAZ:v1p128/r3) supports this position. IBNABIHATIM (source note IBNABIHATIM:v3p961/r1) supports this position. THALABI (source note THALABI:v3p314/r3) supports this position. THALABI (source note THALABI:v3p314/r4) supports this position. MAWARDI-FULL (source note MAWARDI-FULL:v1p490/r2) supports this position. [26 notes: RAZI-FULL(ابن عباس,ابن مسعود وابن عمر والشعبي والنخعي والشافعي); QURTUBI-FULL; BAYDAWI-FULL; ABUHAYYAN-FULL(الزمخشري,علي وابن عباس والحسن ومجاهد وقتادة); IBNKATHIR-FULL(ابن عباس); DURR-FULL(أبو عبيدة,الحسن,الشعبي); ALUSI-FULL(بعض المحققين,علي وابن عباس والحسن); ASAD-NOTES; KURANYOLU-TEFSIR; QIRAAT-ER(Ḥanafī); MUJAHID(الحسن); MUQATIL; MAJAZ; IBNABIHATIM(ابن عباس); THALABI(ابن عباس,ابن عباس والحسن ومجاهد وقتادة); MAWARDI-FULL(علي، ابن عباس، الحسن، قتادة، مجاهد); IBNASHUR-FULL+; TABRISI+]
- 4:43/q19/p3 A touch related sense — TABRISI, d. 548 (author): Touching and mutual touch have the same meaning, since one cannot touch a woman without her touching him. QURTUBI-FULL, d. 671 (الشافعي ومحمد بن عبد الحكم): Al-Shafiʿi and Muhammad b. ʿAbd al-Hakam count anything from either outlet, even if unusual, and include touching. ABUHAYYAN-FULL, d. 745 (author): Junub may derive from distance, since one is apart from purity, or from the side, since one lies with or touches another by his side. SAMIN-DURR, d. 756 (author): جنب derives from جنابة, ‘distance’; a person is so called either for being distant from purity or for lying beside and touching another. IBNKATHIR-FULL, d. 774 (author): The phrase is recited in two forms: lamastum and lamastum (with the long vowel). Exegetes and jurists differ over its meaning. BIQAI-FULL, d. 885 (author): The cited wording about touching women, with references to 4:43 and 5:6, is another example attached to the preceding discussion of literal and figurative usage. | mentions 4:43, 5:6 DURR-FULL, d. 911 (ابن مسعود): Ibn Masud also glosses touching women as ghamz, pressing or squeezing. IBNASHUR-FULL, d. 1393 (author): Variants such as yathurna/yattahharna and lamastum/lamastum increase the possible meanings of a verse. IBNASHUR-FULL, d. 1393 (حمزة والكسائي وخلف): Most readers recite lāmastum with an alif; Hamza, al-Kisaʾi, and Khalaf recite lamastum without one. KURANYOLU-TEFSIR (author): After intending tayammum, one touches clean soil, clay, sand, or stone with the palms, wipes the face, touches again, then wipes arms to the elbows. QIRAAT-ER (Ḥamza and Kisāʾī): Ḥamza and Kisāʾī read Form I lamasa instead of Form III lāmasa, removing the reciprocal morpheme and indicating one-sided touching. STUDYQURAN (legal scholars): Legal scholars hold that major ritual impurity also prohibits touching or reciting the Quran. [12 notes: TABRISI; QURTUBI-FULL(الشافعي ومحمد بن عبد الحكم); ABUHAYYAN-FULL; SAMIN-DURR; IBNKATHIR-FULL; BIQAI-FULL; DURR-FULL(ابن مسعود); IBNASHUR-FULL(حمزة والكسائي وخلف); KURANYOLU-TEFSIR; QIRAAT-ER(Ḥamza and Kisāʾī); STUDYQURAN(legal scholars)]
- 4:43/q19/p4 Bodily touch short of intercourse — TABRISI, d. 548 (عمر بن الخطاب وابن مسعود والشعبي وعطاء): Another interpretation takes it as touching a woman by hand or otherwise; al-Shafiʿi adopts this view. TABRISI, d. 548 (author): Touch first referred to the hand and then broadened beyond literal contact; iltamas means to seek. IBNJAWZI-ZAD, d. 597 (ابن مسعود، ابن عمر، الشعبي، عبيدة، عطاء، ابن سيرين، النخعي، النهدي، الحكم، حماد): Another legal interpretation takes the phrase as touching a woman with the hand. IBNJAWZI-ZAD, d. 597 (أبو علي): Abū ʿAlī says touch can mean contact by hand but is extended to other kinds of contact; 72:8 uses “we touched the heaven” for attempting to reach its secrets. | mentions 72:8 IBNJAWZI-ZAD, d. 597 (أبو علي): The wording “they touched it with their hands” in 6:7 specifies hands to distinguish literal touching from the extended sense; likewise “sons from your loins” in 4:23 distinguishes biological sons from merely named sons. | mentions 6:7, 4:23 QURTUBI-FULL, d. 671 (author): The Maliki nullifiers fall into three kinds; Hanafis count impure bodily emissions regardless of outlet but exclude touching. QURTUBI-FULL, d. 671 (عائشة): When ʿUrwa asked which wife the Prophet kissed, ʿAisha laughed, leaving open that she herself was the one. QURTUBI-FULL, d. 671 (الدارقطني): The kiss report used by Abu Hanifa is mursal: Daraqutni notes that Habib b. Abi Thabit did not hear from ʿUrwa, as Yahya b. Saʿid says Sufyan al-Thawri maintained. QURTUBI-FULL, d. 671 (عائشة): Aisha reports that while searching for the Prophet at night in the mosque, her hand touched the soles of his feet as he prayed in prostration. QURTUBI-FULL, d. 671 (author): Scholars agree that tayammum does not remove major or minor ritual impurity; finding water restores the person to the prior state, based on the Prophet's instruction to Abu Dharr to let water touch his skin. ABUHAYYAN-FULL, d. 745 (أبو عمر): Abū ʿUmar says no regional jurists adopted the hand-touch view, citing reports about tayammum for a junub from ʿAmmār, Abū Dharr, and ʿImrān ibn Ḥuṣayn. IBNKATHIR-FULL, d. 774 (author): An unattributed poem uses ‘I touched the palm of his hand’ to illustrate the physical-touch sense of the verb. IBNKATHIR-FULL, d. 774 (أبو ذر): The Prophet says clean earth purifies a Muslim even after ten years without water; once water is available, the person should let it touch the skin. Al-Tirmidhi grades the report hasan sahih, and Ibn Hibban authenticates it. DURR-FULL, d. 911 (حماد): Hammad says anything touched by one's hands counts as saʿid, even dust from a saddle pad, and may be used for tayammum. DURR-FULL, d. 911 (أبو عثمان): Abu Uthman interprets the contact as touching by hand. ALUSI-FULL, d. 1270 (الجلال المحلي والكمال ابن أبي شريف): Al-Jalal al-Mahalli calls touching by hand literal; Ibn Abi Sharif says this exemplifies one kind of touch rather than defining the full literal meaning. IBNASHUR-FULL, d. 1393 (author): Touching earth with the hands evokes purification because earth is the source of water and is also used to clean vessels, garments, bodies, and through istijmar; it reminds the waterless person to seek water once able. ASAD-NOTES, d. 1412 (author): Tayammum is performed by touching earth or a dust-bearing substance with the palms, then lightly wiping the face and hands. QIRAAT-ER (Shāfiʿī): The Shāfiʿī view takes Form I as one-sided touching, so any skin contact breaks wuḍūʾ. [19 notes: TABRISI(عمر بن الخطاب وابن مسعود والشعبي وعطاء); IBNJAWZI-ZAD(أبو علي,ابن مسعود، ابن عمر، الشعبي، عبيدة، عطاء، ابن سيرين، النخعي، النهدي، الحكم، حماد); QURTUBI-FULL(الدارقطني,عائشة); ABUHAYYAN-FULL(أبو عمر); IBNKATHIR-FULL(أبو ذر); DURR-FULL(أبو عثمان,حماد); ALUSI-FULL(الجلال المحلي والكمال ابن أبي شريف); IBNASHUR-FULL; ASAD-NOTES; QIRAAT-ER(Shāfiʿī)]

## 4:43/q20 When does contact with a woman invalidate ablution?  [لَٰمَسْتُمُ ٱلنِّسَآءَ · law]
turns on: The cited interpretations, reports, language arguments, and legal evidence differ.
- 4:43/q20/p1 Any direct bodily contact invalidates ablution — IBNATIYYA-FULL, d. 542 (الجمهور): The majority requires wiping the whole face within its known ablution boundaries, carefully ensuring no part is left untouched. QURTUBI-FULL, d. 671 (author): Al-ghaʾit covers minor ablution nullifiers; the strongest Maliki classification groups them as loss of reason, customary discharge, and touching. QURTUBI-FULL, d. 671 (أبو حنيفة): Abu Hanifa takes mulamasa to mean intercourse alone: a junub person may tayammum, but hand-touch and a pleasurable kiss do not invalidate ablution. QURTUBI-FULL, d. 671 (عائشة): Hanafis cite a Daraqutni report from ʿAisha that the Prophet kissed a wife and prayed without renewing ablution. QURTUBI-FULL, d. 671 (علي بن زياد): Ali b. Ziyad says touching through a thick garment has no effect, but touching through a thin one requires ablution. QURTUBI-FULL, d. 671 (author): Al-Qurtubi calls the Maliki position strongest: touch short of intercourse invalidates ablution, as reported from ʿUmar, his son, and Ibn Masʿud and held by most jurists. QURTUBI-FULL, d. 671 (الشافعي): Al-Shafiʿi's view that uncovered skin-contact with a woman breaks ablution follows the apparent wording, whether he touches her or she touches him. QURTUBI-FULL, d. 671 (الشافعي): Al-Qurtubi says it is good precaution to perform ablution after touching a woman's hair. QURTUBI-FULL, d. 671 (author): The claim that the mere act of touching invalidates ablution would also make a man lose ablution by slapping his wife to discipline her, an implication Qurtubi says no one accepts. QURTUBI-FULL, d. 671 (الشافعي): One Shafi'i opinion says touching even a little girl invalidates ablution based on the word “women”; Qurtubi rejects it, likening a child's touch to touching a wall. QURTUBI-FULL, d. 671 (الأوزاعي): Al-Awza'i says he would advise a man who asks about kissing his wife to renew ablution, but would not blame him if he did not. BAYDAWI-FULL, d. 685 (author): The phrase may mean skin touching skin; al-Shāfiʿī uses it as evidence that touching women invalidates ablution. IBNKATHIR-FULL, d. 774 (آخرون): Other interpreters take the phrase to mean any bodily touching, requiring wudu when any part of a man’s body touches a woman’s body directly. IBNKATHIR-FULL, d. 774 (عبد الله بن مسعود): Ibn Masud says touching means contact short of intercourse; he also reports that a kiss counts as touch and requires wudu. IBNKATHIR-FULL, d. 774 (ابن عمر): Ibn Umar performed wudu after kissing a woman and considered the kiss part of al-limas; Ibn Kathir also cites Ibn Umar’s statement that kissing or touching one’s wife by hand requires wudu. IBNKATHIR-FULL, d. 774 (عمر بن الخطاب): Al-Daraqutni transmits from Umar a report similar to requiring wudu after touch; another report says Umar kissed his wife and prayed without wudu, so Ibn Kathir says the former, if sound, may indicate recommendation. IBNKATHIR-FULL, d. 774 (الشافعي وأصحابه ومالك وأحمد بن حنبل): Requiring wudu after physical touch is the position of al-Shafi‘i and his followers, Malik, and the well-known position from Ahmad. IBNKATHIR-FULL, d. 774 (معاذ): In a report from Muadh, a man says he met a woman and did everything short of intercourse; Quran 11:114 was revealed, and the Prophet ordered him to perform wudu and pray, saying the ruling applies to all believers. Al-Tirmidhi calls the report disconnected and al-Nasa’i transmits it as mursal. | mentions 11:114 IBNKATHIR-FULL, d. 774 (ابن جرير): Ibn Jarir prefers intercourse as the meaning, citing a report that the Prophet kissed one of his wives and then prayed without renewing wudu. IBNKATHIR-FULL, d. 774 (عائشة): Aisha reports that the Prophet kissed one of his wives, then went out to pray without renewing wudu; when asked who the wife was, she smiled. Abu Dawud, al-Tirmidhi, and Ibn Majah transmit it. IBNKATHIR-FULL, d. 774 (عائشة): Aisha reports that the Prophet kissed her after wudu and did not repeat it; a route through Ibrahim al-Taymi is also transmitted, but Abu Dawud and al-Nasa’i say Ibrahim did not hear from her. IBNKATHIR-FULL, d. 774 (أم سلمة): Umm Salama reports that the Prophet kissed her while fasting, did not break his fast, and did not renew wudu. IBNKATHIR-FULL, d. 774 (زينب السهمية): Zaynab al-Sahmiyya reports that the Prophet would kiss and then pray without performing wudu; Ahmad also transmits a route through Aisha. DURR-FULL, d. 911 (ابن مسعود): Ibn Masud explains touching women as contact short of intercourse, including kissing, and says it requires ablution. DURR-FULL, d. 911 (ابن عمر): Ibn Umar used to perform ablution after kissing a woman, saying that a kiss is touching. DURR-FULL, d. 911 (ابن عمر): Ibn Umar says kissing one's wife or touching her by hand is contact that requires ablution. DURR-FULL, d. 911 (عمر): Umar says kissing is a form of touching and requires ablution. DURR-FULL, d. 911 (محمد بن سيرين): Muhammad ibn Sirin says he was told Ibn Umar performed ablution after touching his private part; he took this and Ubayda's gesture to be the same interpretation. DURR-FULL, d. 911 (أبو ذر): Abu Dharr reports the Prophet saying pure earth serves as a Muslim's ablution for up to ten years without water; when water is found, it should touch the skin. ALUSI-FULL, d. 1270 (الشافعي والزهري والأوزاعي): Al-Shafiʿi infers that touching a woman invalidates ablution; al-Zuhri and al-Awzaʿi agree. ALUSI-FULL, d. 1270 (عائشة): Aisha reports placing her hand on the Prophet's feet while he prostrated, evidence cited against invalidating ablution for the person touched. ELMALILI, d. 1361 (Zâhiriyye): The Zahiris hold that mere touch invalidates ablution, but only for the person who touches, not the person touched. IBNASHUR-FULL, d. 1393 (الشافعي): Al-Shafiʿi reads the phrase literally and makes a man's touching a woman's body with his hand invalidate wudu; Ibn Ashur calls this an implausible reading. IBNASHUR-FULL, d. 1393 (جمهور العلماء): The majority, including Malik and Abu Hanifa, do not require wudu after touching one's wife unless something is emitted. [78 notes: IBNATIYYA-FULL(أبو حنيفة,ابن كثير ونافع وأبو عمرو وابن عامر وعاصم وحمزة والكسائي,الجمهور,الشافعي,عمر وعبد الله بن مسعود وغيرهما,قتادة والزهري,مالك); QURTUBI-FULL(أبو حنيفة,الأوزاعي,الشافعي,عائشة,علي بن زياد); BAYDAWI-FULL; IBNKATHIR-FULL(آخرون,أم سلمة,ابن عمر,الشافعي وأصحابه ومالك وأحمد بن حنبل,زينب السهمية,عائشة,عبد الله بن مسعود,عمر بن الخطاب,معاذ); DURR-FULL(أبو ذر,ابن عمر,ابن مسعود,عمر,محمد بن سيرين); ALUSI-FULL(الشافعي والزهري والأوزاعي,عائشة); ELMALILI(Zâhiriyye); IBNASHUR-FULL(الشافعي,جمهور العلماء); THALABI(أبو حنيفة وأبو يوسف,أبو قتادة,أبيه,ابن عباس والحسن البصري ومحمد بن الحسين,الأوزاعي,الثوري,الجمهور,الشافعي,الشافعي وابن مسعود وابن عمر والزهري وربيعة,الليث وربيعة,حذيفة,عائشة,مالك,مالك والليث بن سعد وأحمد بن حنبل وإسحاق بن راهويه,معاذ); BAGHAWI-FULL(أبو حنيفة,ابن عباس والحسن والثوري,ابن مسعود وابن عمر والزهري والأوزاعي والشافعي,سفيان الثوري وابن المبارك وأصحاب الرأي وأحمد وإسحاق,عائشة,مالك والليث بن سعد وأحمد وإسحاق); IBNKATHIR-FULL(ابن جرير)+; QURTUBI-FULL+]
- 4:43/q20/p2 Ordinary touch does not invalidate ablution — RAZI-FULL, d. 606 (ابن عباس والحسن ومجاهد وقتادة وأبو حنيفة): Ibn ʿAbbās, al-Ḥasan, Mujāhid, Qatāda, and Abū Ḥanīfa take touching women to mean intercourse, since touching by hand does not invalidate ablution. RAZI-FULL, d. 606 (أهل الظاهر): The literalists hold that only the person who touches a woman loses ablution; the person touched does not. QURTUBI-FULL, d. 671 (مالك): Malik says intercourse calls for tayammum; hand-touch calls for ablution if pleasure is felt, while touch without desire does not, a view also held by Ahmad and Ishaq. QURTUBI-FULL, d. 671 (الأوزاعي): Al-Awzaʿi says hand-touch invalidates ablution but other bodily contact does not, citing the verse about touching with their hands. | mentions 6:7 QURTUBI-FULL, d. 671 (الشافعي): In al-Shafiʿi's view, touching hair, teeth, or nails does not break ablution; precaution favors ablution after touching hair. QURTUBI-FULL, d. 671 (الشافعي): Touching through clothing does not break ablution even with pleasure; direct skin contact counts whether deliberate or forgetful and whether the unrelated woman is alive or dead. QURTUBI-FULL, d. 671 (الشافعي): Al-Shafiʿi has two views on touching a young girl, elderly woman, or unmarriageable female: it breaks ablution under the verse's general wording, or does not because desire is irrelevant to them. QURTUBI-FULL, d. 671 (المروزي): Al-Marwazi prefers the unrestricted Shafiʿi view because the verse does not condition touch on desire, as also reflected by Companions and most Successors. QURTUBI-FULL, d. 671 (author): Al-Qurtubi rejects the objection that the Prophet could not touch ʿAisha through clothing: the report expressly describes him as touching her, and the Sunnah specifies that touch without desire or intent does not invalidate ablution. QURTUBI-FULL, d. 671 (author): The reports show that direct touch does not invalidate ablution for every person who touches; Qurtubi's rule distinguishes touch according to pleasure. QURTUBI-FULL, d. 671 (أبو ثور): Abu Thawr holds that kissing, intimate contact, or touching one's wife does not require ablution; Qurtubi associates this with the Hanafi school. ABUHAYYAN-FULL, d. 745 (أبو حنيفة): Abū Ḥanīfa reads the phrase as intercourse, not hand-touch; even a kiss with desire does not invalidate ablution in his school. IBNKATHIR-FULL, d. 774 (author): The report does not establish wudu for touching because Abu Layla did not meet Muadh, leaving a gap in the chain. ALUSI-FULL, d. 1270 (أبو حنيفة): Abu Hanifa holds that touching does not invalidate ablution, even with desire; another qualification says it does if erection occurs. ALUSI-FULL, d. 1270 (الشافعي): Al-Shafiʿi differed over touching close relatives or a young unrelated woman; his sounder view is that it does not invalidate ablution, like touching teeth, nails, or hair. ALUSI-FULL, d. 1270 (الشافعي): Al-Shafiʿi does not invalidate the touched person's ablution when their private part is touched, since that person did not touch a locus of pleasure. ELMALILI, d. 1361 (author): The Hanafi view, followed by Elmalılı, excludes mere skin contact from the meaning; touching a woman's body alone does not invalidate ablution. KURANYOLU-TEFSIR (Mâlik, Ahmed, İshak): Malik, Ahmad, and Ishaq hold that touching with sexual pleasure invalidates ablution, while touching without that aim does not. KURANYOLU-TEFSIR (Evzâî): Al-Awza'i holds that touching with the hand invalidates ablution, but touching with another part of the body does not. KURANYOLU-TEFSIR (Şevkânî): Al-Shawkani says the word fits both senses, so neither can determine a ruling; Prophetic and Companion practice shows skin contact alone does not invalidate ablution and intercourse is meant. The commentary endorses this. [20 notes: RAZI-FULL(أهل الظاهر,ابن عباس والحسن ومجاهد وقتادة وأبو حنيفة); QURTUBI-FULL(أبو ثور,الأوزاعي,الشافعي,مالك); ABUHAYYAN-FULL(أبو حنيفة); IBNKATHIR-FULL; ALUSI-FULL(أبو حنيفة,الشافعي); KURANYOLU-TEFSIR(Evzâî,Mâlik, Ahmed, İshak); ELMALILI+; KURANYOLU-TEFSIR(Şevkânî)+; QURTUBI-FULL(المروزي)+]
- 4:43/q20/p3 Touch with desire or intent for pleasure — QURTUBI-FULL, d. 671 (عبد الملك بن الماجشون): Ibn al-Majishun requires ablution for intentionally touching one's wife by hand for play, whether or not pleasure follows. QURTUBI-FULL, d. 671 (القاضي أبو الوليد الباجي): Al-Baji clarifies the Maliki rule: intending pleasure by touching requires ablution whether pleasure actually occurs or not. ABUHAYYAN-FULL, d. 745 (مالك وأحمد وإسحاق): Mālik says intercourse requires tayammum and hand-touch does so only with pleasure; without desire there is no ablution. Aḥmad and Isḥāq agree on the desire distinction. ALUSI-FULL, d. 1270 (مالك والليث بن سعد وأحمد): Malik, al-Layth, and one report from Ahmad say touching invalidates ablution only when accompanied by desire. ALUSI-FULL, d. 1270 (الشافعي): In al-Shafiʿi's stronger view, the person touched also loses ablution because both participants share the possibility of pleasure, as in intercourse. IBNASHUR-FULL, d. 1393 (مالك): Malik treats the phrase as both literal and euphemistic but invalidates wudu for literal touch only with pleasure or intent to take pleasure; Ibn Ashur attributes this to early reports, not the verse's intended sense. STUDYQURAN (legal scholars): Scholars disagree whether “touching women” means intercourse only or any touch, regardless of desire. [7 notes: QURTUBI-FULL(القاضي أبو الوليد الباجي,عبد الملك بن الماجشون); ABUHAYYAN-FULL(مالك وأحمد وإسحاق); ALUSI-FULL(الشافعي,مالك والليث بن سعد وأحمد); IBNASHUR-FULL(مالك); STUDYQURAN(legal scholars)]
- 4:43/q20/p4 Direct skin contact invalidates both — RAZI-FULL, d. 606 (الشافعي): Al-Shāfiʿī holds that both the toucher and the person touched lose ablution. [1 notes: RAZI-FULL(الشافعي)]
- 4:43/q20/p5 Only arousing contact that causes erection — QURTUBI-FULL, d. 671 (مالك): Ibn Nafiʿ transmits from Malik that erection alone requires neither ablution nor washing the penis unless accompanied by touch or pre-seminal fluid; Abu Ishaq says erection alone breaks ablution, citing the Mudawwana. [1 notes: QURTUBI-FULL(مالك)]
- 4:43/q20/p6 Only hand contact — QURTUBI-FULL, d. 671 (الأوزاعي): Al-Awza'i limits legally relevant touch to the hand because that is the organ most commonly meant by the term; foot contact with a wife's private parts or belly would not invalidate ablution. [1 notes: QURTUBI-FULL(الأوزاعي)]

- 4:43/q21/p6 junuban is coordinated with the circumstantial phrase wa-antum sukārā, both being accusative states: do not pray drunk or in major impurity — NASAFI-FULL, d. 710 (author): Junuban is coordinated with the circumstantial phrase wa-antum sukārā, both being accusative states: do not pray drunk or in major impurity.
